He was already running it through his head, she could tell — not the personal risk calculation but the structural one, the load path of the operation, where the stress would be and where the material was sufficient to hold it. This was how Boiler thought about everything. She had known this for four years and had worked with it for four years and it was one of the more consistent and reliable things she had encountered in this business.

"What's the scout on the building?" he said.

"East camera on a non-standard angle. Civic maintenance conduit under the north foundation. Scout has both threading access points mapped."

"She's threading from outside?"

"Yes."

"Good." A simple professional assessment. "Does she need me on the conduit?"

"She said she can manage it from the freight lane position."

"All right." He looked at the structural tablet briefly, at the load model running its iteration. "Send me the building schematic. I want to look at the freight lane geometry before tomorrow."

She transferred the schematic. He received it and set the tablet to add the data.

"Boiler," she said.

He looked up.

"The community center. The load path you were describing. What replaces the Lotus dependency if it changes?"

He was quiet for a moment. Not evasive — the question required the honest answer and the honest answer required a moment to locate. "Nothing, right now," he said. "You'd need to build something. Not destroy the existing structure and then build — you'd have to build the replacement load path while the old structure was still standing, so the weight transferred gradually rather than dropped. That's how you do it structurally. You don't remove a load-bearing element until you've confirmed the new element is load-rated and in position."

"Has anyone tried?"

"Not systematically. Adaeze's cooperative does it on a service-by-service basis. Legal services, health records, some of the debt challenge work. But not the primary Syndicate dependency — the supply chains, the market pricing, the direct community credit structures. That would require scale and funding that the cooperative doesn't have." He paused. "Would require scale and funding. Present tense."

She looked at the wall. The three pour dates, the three eras of repair, the forty years of a building that had been worth maintaining because the people using it had continued to use it and continue to find it worth maintaining.

"Briefing tomorrow afternoon," she said. "I'll send the location tonight."

He returned to the wall. She descended the scaffold.

The building held the specific afternoon quiet of a working space where the work was real — not performance, not waiting, but active problem-solving in a medium that didn't allow shortcuts. She walked through the east wing on her way out and looked at the ceiling joints, the original 2178 pour overhead and the settlement visible if you knew how to look for it.

She knew what held and what released under pressure. She had been learning it in a different vocabulary for a long time.

She let herself out through the ground-floor exit and walked north into the buffer zone.

## The Market Run

[Beat 8]
The freight depot conversion looked, from the street, like someone had decided that the best way to make industrial bones interesting was to not cover them. The original structure was intact: the loading bay arches, the iron overhead crane track still running the length of the upper floor, the exposed cable conduit bundles on the ceiling that belonged to a pre-conversion electrical infrastructure and had been left as aesthetic choices by whoever had done the fit-out. The glass-and-composite entrance pavilion was the only addition that announced anything had changed. The bones showed and the bones were the point.

Rook was on the far side of the street, thirty meters south of the entrance, at a public seating node outside a transit stop. She had a sight line to the pavilion entrance and no sight line to the east face, which was Scout's domain. She was not in the building. The operation did not require her in the building.

"Positions," she said, sub-vocal, into the team channel.

Boiler confirmed from his position on the street perimeter, north of the entrance at the block corner. Ekow confirmed from the south approach, covering the secondary route from the transit node that a fast exit might use if the primary exit became complicated. Scout confirmed from the freight lane — she had been in position for twenty minutes already and had threaded the east camera loop on the building's security network. Half-second reconnect window in the Meridian HD-7 handshake. She had tested the loop twice without triggering any alert response from the building's security monitoring.

"Building security is running the standard twelve-minute rotation," Scout said, in the team channel. "Two staff inside. The one at the entrance checked provenance documentation on the last two buyers who came in and logged it, which means they're running a registration system on today's traffic. I've got the building's internal logging feed — they're not pushing to external civic storage, it's local only."

"Local only means no civic record," Vox said, from outside the team's physical footprint entirely, running her four channels from her Z4 workspace. "Tran-Okonkwo is not filing a public transaction record for today's sales."

"He's keeping the provenance documentation internal," Rook said.

"Yes. Which is consistent with the stolen acquisition. He knows what he has and he knows the paper trail is not in his favor, so he's running a private record that he controls."

Rook noted this. "Lace."

"Ready," Lace said. She had been ready for twenty minutes, in the transit node bathroom, running the final verification of her cover — the documentation package she had assembled over the past day, the buyer identity she had built, the provenance chain she was carrying for a collection that had a verifiable history and a specific taste in mid-market Z3/Z4 hybrid art that made interest in a piece like the Headcount sculpture both plausible and financially credible.

"You're clear to enter," Rook said.

She watched Lace emerge from the transit node and walk toward the pavilion entrance with the specific quality that Lace produced when she was being someone else — a slightly different gait, a slightly different way of carrying her coat, the small modulations of posture and pace that constituted a different person's relationship to public space. This was not performance in the theatrical sense. It was inhabitation. Lace did not play buyers; she became the buyer she had constructed, which was a distinction that mattered because collectors and their staff read the people they dealt with carefully and people who were performing could be read.

The entrance staff checked her documentation. A brief exchange — Rook could not hear the words at this distance but she could read the interaction's body language: documents presented, examined with the attention appropriate to the documentation's apparent quality, a comment or question from the staff, Lace's unhurried reply. The staff returned the documents. Lace entered.

"I'm in," Lace said. "Two staff on the floor — one at the entrance, one at the far end working the upper-level displays. The floor is set up in dealer format, pieces in curated groupings, the serious work on the second level. I can see the central installation space from here. There are four pieces under exhibition lighting."

"Is the Headcount piece one of them?"

A pause. "Yes." A different quality in the pause than anything that had preceded it. "It's a significant physical installation. I understand why someone wanted it back."

Rook waited.

"Ceramic composite housing," Lace said. "Roughly half a meter on the long axis, maybe thirty centimeters tall. Dark glaze, high finish. The exterior surface has been worked — there are embedded markings, very small, running the length of the piece in a repeating pattern that looks typographic but isn't standard character sets. The data layer is probably accessible from the south face — there's a reading node housing at the base, the kind of port you use for embedded provenance documentation." Another pause. "It's displayed on a plinth under independent lighting. Not grouped with other pieces. The lighting is the statement: it's the primary piece, the others are context."

"Tran-Okonkwo?"

"I see him. He's talking with the other staff member on the upper level. He'll come down."

Rook watched the building entrance. The iron overhead crane track was visible through the pavilion glass — the original industrial element crossing the upper space, the collector's market arranged beneath it as if the track were an architectural feature rather than what it had been: a mechanism for moving heavy loads through a space designed for heavy loads. The bones showed. The bones were doing something different now.

"He's coming down," Lace said.

She felt the operation shift into its more precise register. Everything before had been positioning; this was the actual work. She turned her attention to the building's exits and her own sight lines and let Lace work.

---

Yusuf Tran-Okonkwo was, by Lace's sub-vocal relay to the team channel, approximately fifty, Z3 collector in his bearing and manner, entirely aware of the fact that he was in possession of something people wanted. He greeted her with the specific warmth of a dealer who knew what they had and was therefore in no hurry — friendly, professional, willing to give the conversation all the time it needed because time was not his constraint.

"You're here for the Headcount piece," he said. Not a question.

"I saw the inquiry go through the Z3 dealer network," Lace said. She was not pretending not to be there for the piece. Pretending was the approach that collectors read as evasion. She was being honest about her interest and careful about her position, which was a different orientation that produced better results. "It matches a specific gap in the collection I'm building."

"And what is the collection you're building?"

She explained the collection — the buyer identity she had constructed, the aesthetic direction, the three pieces she had acquired in the past eight months that established the thematic frame. She had done this work completely enough that the explanation required no performance. The collection existed in her prepared documentation and in her prepared mind, each piece cross-referenced, the thematic rationale real enough to sustain any question he could ask about it.

Tran-Okonkwo listened with his full attention. He was reading her. She let him.

"What's your budget?" he said.

"What's your ask?"

A small smile. This was not a new dance. "I paid sixty-two hundred. The piece has appreciated since I acquired it. The inquiry this week came in at nine thousand. I haven't responded."

"Nine thousand is the current market ceiling for a piece of this category at this stage in the artist's career," Lace said. "I can offer eight-five, with standard civic provenance documentation."

"The documentation is the interesting part," he said. "The piece came to me through informal channels."

"I know where it came from," Lace said. "The documentation I provide would be clean. That's not a problem for the collection I'm building."

He considered her for a moment. "You know where it came from and you want it anyway."

"The piece is worth having. The provenance gap is a solvable administrative issue."

He walked to the plinth. The Headcount sculpture was exactly what Lace had described: ceramic composite, worked surface, the typographic markings running the length of the dark glaze in a pattern that was almost-language without being language. Under the dedicated lighting it had a specific presence — not decorative, not ambient. Something that required looking at rather than looking past.

Rook could not see this from her position outside. She was reading it through Lace's relay and through the quality of what was not being said on the team channel.

"The piece is not for eight-five," Tran-Okonkwo said. He was looking at the sculpture rather than at Lace. "I have a buyer at nine thousand who wants it for institutional display. You're offering more than the piece's standard market value in this format, which tells me something about what you think the piece actually is. The institutional buyer is not interested in that." He turned to look at her. "I'm interested in that. But not for a discount from someone who already knows the value."

"What is your number?" Lace said.

"Twelve thousand."

The team channel was very quiet.

"He knows," Boiler said, in the sub-vocal register, to Rook rather than the full channel.

"He doesn't know what it is," Rook said. "He knows it's worth more than market."

"That's enough," Ekow said.

Lace was silent for a moment, which was Lace's indication that she was processing the situation rather than that she had run out of moves. She had not run out of moves. She was deciding which move to make.

"I need to confirm with the collection," she said, to Tran-Okonkwo. "Twelve is above the single-acquisition ceiling I have authorization for. Let me make a call."

"Of course." He moved toward his staff colleague on the upper level. A polite, professional withdrawal. The art market ran on the expectation of consultation.

"He's walking away," Lace confirmed, in the team channel. "I have maybe four minutes before he expects an answer."

Rook ran the arithmetic. The operation budget had not been built for a twelve-thousand acquisition. The initial contract from Ferris was twelve thousand total — using the full contract payout on the acquisition left the crew with zero margin and zero return for the operation overhead and risk. There were other options. She assessed them in order.

"Scout," she said. "The reading node at the base of the plinth. Can you pull the data layer remotely?"

"Not without physical contact with the node or a threading access point inside the installation space. I'd need to be in the building."

"Vox. The local logging system. Can you access Tran-Okonkwo's transaction records?"

"I have the local log feed Scout threaded earlier. What do you need?"

"His current pending offers. Anything logged in the last seventy-two hours."

A brief pause. "The nine-thousand offer is logged — a Z3 institutional buyer, filed this morning, no deadline noted. There's a second offer logged forty hours ago for eight thousand from a private buyer. No flags, no identity attached beyond a Z4 address." Another pause. "And there's a third log entry from four days ago. It's not an offer — it's a notation. Three words: 'Worth the wait.'"

The channel was quiet.

"Worth the wait," Rook said.

"Yes."

He was not waiting for a twelve-thousand buyer. He was waiting for the buyer who would tell him what the piece was. The price was a filter. He was going to sell to whoever proved they knew what they were actually acquiring.

"Lace," Rook said. "He wants to know someone else knows what it is. Give him that without giving him the data."

A pause. "That is a very specific instruction."

"I know. You can do it."

"I know I can do it," Lace said, without inflection. "I'm just noting it was specific."

Rook watched the building. The iron crane track above the pavilion glass. The heavy loads the building had been designed to carry, repurposed now to carry something else, the track still running its original geometry above the changed function below.

"He's coming back," Lace said.

Rook heard Lace shift her approach. Not in words — there were no words on the channel while the conversation inside was happening. She heard it in the spacing of Lace's transmissions, the quality of what arrived and when, the economy of someone who had shifted from one register to another and was now doing something she was very good at.

"The collection I'm building," Lace told Tran-Okonkwo, "is not about individual pieces. It's about what the pieces are proxies for. What they contain that isn't visible on the surface."

A moment.

"Tell me what you think this contains," he said.

"I think it contains fifteen years of something the artist was paid to make invisible," Lace said. "I think the title is not ironic."

Silence in the building, audible as the absence of Lace's transmission for four seconds.

"Twelve thousand," Tran-Okonkwo said. "And you take it tonight."

"We'll take it now," Lace said.

[Beat 9]
The transaction took eleven minutes. Rook counted them from the public seating node across the street, watching the pavilion glass and letting the team channel run at low priority while the arithmetic of the extraction settled into its operational shape.

Tran-Okonkwo provided packaging. This was expected: a collector's market at his tier supplied transport infrastructure as part of the sale, both because the pieces were valuable enough to warrant it and because providing the packaging meant the seller controlled the exit logistics. What arrived from the storage room behind the upper level was a handled transport case — rigid composite, foam interior, sealed latch hardware. The kind of case that announced its contents as something worth carrying carefully without announcing what those contents specifically were.

"He's handling the case himself," Lace said over the team channel, sub-vocal, her voice arriving in Rook's ear as if she were standing close. "The staff member is logging the transaction on the internal system. Tran-Okonkwo is packing the piece personally. He's careful."

"He should be careful," Ekow said. He was on the south approach, covering the transit node secondary route. His voice on the channel carried the same quality as his words in person: direct and uninflected, the fewest syllables that would carry the meaning. "He paid sixty-two hundred for it."

"He's selling it for twelve thousand," Boiler said, from the north perimeter. "He's not careful about the money. He's careful about the object."

Rook processed this. Boiler was right. Tran-Okonkwo had been attentive to the sculpture in a way that was distinct from the attentiveness of a man protecting an investment. When the conversation had reached its resolution point he had walked to the plinth and looked at the piece for a moment before speaking his number. That was not a seller calculating margins. That was a man who understood, at some level, that the object had a quality his calculation was only adjacent to.

Worth the wait, he had written in his private log four days ago. He had been waiting for someone who knew what they were acquiring.

She had counted the operational windows. The interior staff member ran a logging rotation with a two-minute gap after each transaction entry, during which the monitoring bandwidth on the door-facing camera dropped to passive mode. Scout had found this twenty minutes ago in the building's internal feed. The gap was a standard efficiency trade-off in collector market security architecture — processor load management — producing a predictable window. Two minutes was enough.

"He's closing the case," Lace said. "The latch sequence is manual, three-point."

"When you're ready," Rook said.

"I'm ready."

"Out through the main entrance. I want you on the street before the logging rotation resets."

"I know."

She watched the pavilion entrance. The staff member at the door position was processing something on their tablet, attention directed inward. The street traffic was mid-afternoon standard: nothing that read as surveillance or threat, the pedestrian density useful cover and nothing else.

Lace came through the entrance.

She walked the way Lace walked when she was carrying something that mattered — steady pace, the handled case at her right side, no adjustment to her carriage that would suggest weight was a concern. The case was held at shoulder-level in her grip, the rigid housing doing the structural work. She moved like someone who had done this before and expected to do it again. The entrance staff glanced at her. Their attention continued past her.

"Clear," Scout said.

"Peel off the freight lane," Rook said. "Join from the south side."

Scout came off the freight lane in her maintenance contractor cover and fell into the pedestrian flow behind Lace at a distance of twelve meters — close enough to be immediate support, far enough to be a different pedestrian entirely. The crawler case she carried as a prop merged without friction into the street traffic.

"Boiler," Rook said.

"Walking. North to east on the arterial."

"Ekow."

"South route. I'll meet at the transit node."

Rook stood from the seating node and entered the pedestrian flow headed east. She was forty meters behind Lace and Scout. The sight line was maintained without effort — the transport case was visible above the crowd at irregular intervals, the handled format and Lace's particular way of carrying things distinguishable from the ambient traffic.

They turned east at the arterial junction. The transit node was two blocks. The vehicle Vox had arranged was at the south transit bay, logged as a contract courier pickup under a Z3 commercial registration that had been used twice before and would not be reused after today.

"Vehicle confirms," Vox said. "Bay seven, south transit. Driver's been waiting nineteen minutes."

"Lace is ninety seconds out," Rook said.

The extraction completed without incident. Lace and Scout reached the transit bay first; Ekow arrived forty seconds after, from the south junction; Boiler forty seconds after that, from the east. Rook was last. She stood at the transit node perimeter for three additional minutes before joining the vehicle, watching the entry points and the vehicle itself and confirming what the team channel had already confirmed: no active surveillance on the courier, no coverage following the crew's path from the collector's market.

Clean.

She got in.

The vehicle was sealed courier format — enclosed cargo compartment, no external identification beyond the commercial registration. Lace had placed the transport case on the interior rack. The handled case sat in the cradle designed for transport, the three-point latch housing visible, the contents enclosed.

"Twelve thousand for something we're not delivering," Boiler said. He was looking at the case.

"We haven't decided that yet," Rook said.

Boiler looked at her briefly. The look conveyed that he had already decided, and that he believed she had also already decided, and that what she had just said was not a decision-statement. It was an acknowledgment that they would have the conversation properly, with Vox, when the data layer was open. He did not press it. He looked back at the case.

The vehicle entered traffic.

---

Park Gi-su noted the time: fourteen forty-seven.

He was seven blocks south of the freight depot conversion, in a Z3 commercial building's fourth-floor operations room that the Lotus maintained for administrative work requiring more bandwidth than the Z6 facilities could provide. Two surveillance operators, one analytical terminal. The terminal was his. He sat at it squarely, without unnecessary movement, the joint actuators at his knees operating with the silent economy that Lotus enforcement hardware was built for — iron-quiet under the fabric of his field gear, invisible until they weren't.

The surveillance feed from the freight depot conversion's exterior was one of four he was monitoring simultaneously. The other three were the building approaches — the arterial, the transit node, the service lane running parallel to the freight lane from the south. Standard coverage geometry, modified for the sight-line constraints of the block. He had mapped the gaps in the building's own security camera network on the first day of the watch and had positioned his supplemental nodes to cover them. Three weeks, four days. The cable runs from the supplemental nodes to this room ran through a Z3 civic conduit junction — iron under the street, carrying the load of his surveillance without announcement.

The crew had been good.

Not exceptional. Exceptional crews left no trace and he would not have identified them at all. But better than average, which was worth noting in the operational log. The buyer had entered using legitimate provenance documentation and a cover collection that would withstand three layers of background verification. The threading operation on the east camera — the Meridian HD-7, the handshake delay the model was known for — had been executed cleanly enough that if he had not been looking for it he would have read the log as normal reconnect activity. The freight lane tech had brought a crawler case as cover. The street positions had been professional.

Five operators for an art acquisition. That was the data point that had settled his assessment within the first four minutes.

Collectors sent one or two people, sometimes with an independent carrier. They sent five when they understood that the acquisition was not a collector acquisition. This crew understood what they were acquiring, which meant they were not working for a private buyer. Which meant the anonymous client who had commissioned the Z3 market recovery was either significantly better resourced than a private buyer, or was not the original source of the commission at all.

He tagged the crew's faces in the surveillance log with timestamps and approach vectors. The buyer: female, precise carriage, the small modulations of someone running a social cover rather than walking in her own face. The freight lane tech: female, crawler case, maintenance credentials. The north perimeter: male, large frame. The south approach: male, combat frame, the specific stillness of a body with significant kinetic capacity choosing not to deploy it. The coordinator: female, transit seating node, left last.

He ran the coordinator's face against the Thorn network's identification archive. Partial match to an operator whose name had appeared twice in intelligence reports from the Crimson and Chrome disruption three months prior. Not confirmed. Plausible. He filed the assessment without annotation beyond the match probability.

The directive on the Headcount piece was recovery and return. He had the access and the authorization to close the acquisition at any of three points during the operation's execution — before the buyer entered the building, during the interior transaction, or during the exterior extraction. At any of those points he could have deployed a contact team and recovered the piece without significant operational difficulty.

He had not.

This was not a deviation from the directive. The directive said return the piece. It did not specify that returning the piece was more valuable than the information it produced in transit. He had made the assessment that following whoever came for the sculpture would produce intelligence worth more than the piece itself, and he had filed that assessment with the coordinating Stem forty-two minutes before the crew arrived at the building. The Stem had not responded. In his operational experience, the absence of a countermanding directive was authorization to proceed on the filed assessment.

He watched the transport vehicle exit the transit bay and merge into Z3 arterial traffic.

Three weeks, four days of watch. Sixteen distinct inquiries on the Headcount piece, twelve standard collector market interest, four flagged as operationally significant. This crew was the fifth distinct contact with the piece and the first to complete an acquisition. The first to bring field operators.

They had the sculpture.

Where they took it would tell him what he needed to know. Who was holding it would tell him whether the network pursuing Headcount was a procurement operation or an intelligence operation. Those were different threats and they required different responses. He did not need to make that assessment tonight. He needed to make it when he knew where the sculpture was.

He initiated the transit corridor coverage query that would track the vehicle's route through the Z3 civic sensor network. Passive monitoring — traffic data, not pursuit. No contact team. The query would log the vehicle at each civic intersection, building the route as it developed.

The vehicle turned south at the fourth block.

He noted the direction.

---

Rook watched the city through the courier vehicle's narrow viewport and let the question of the data layer settle into its proper position: open, without answer, awaiting information.

The transport case sat in the interior rack across from her. Three-point latch, closed. The ceramic composite housing held whatever fifteen years of Gray Zone demographics looked like in civic registry schema. She would know what it looked like in twelve minutes.

"Safe house confirms," Vox said in her ear. "Clean approach, no coverage activity in the last four hours."

"Good," Rook said.

She was aware, at the edge of the thing she was not yet letting herself think about, that the question of delivery had already shifted. Not because she had decided not to deliver. Because Vox had told her the schema format, and Ekow had gone quiet in a specific way when she had named it, and the operational brief said twelve thousand Quanta for a courier recovery — and she had worked in this city long enough to know that nobody paid twelve thousand for a provenance chain.

Whatever was in the sculpture was the point.

She looked at the case and waited to find out what the point was.

[Beat 10]
The safe house was a Z4 commercial unit on the second floor of a converted warehouse building, leased under a holding company that Vox had been maintaining for two years. Three rooms: an operational workspace, a secondary with basic facilities, and a storage room with a lock that required two separate key sequences. The operational workspace had a workstation array, a cleared table, good light, and the specific quality of air in rooms that were used regularly and maintained carefully — not comfortable, not institutional, the precise absence of both.

They arrived in sequence with a seven-minute stagger between vehicles. Vox was already there when Rook came in last — she had arrived first, which was Vox's practice when a data operation was the immediate next task. The workstation array was running. Four channels, the streams active.

The transport case was on the cleared table. Lace had carried it through without ceremony and set it down. Three-point latch, closed.

"Reading interface first," Vox said, by way of a general briefing that was also just the order of operations. She moved to the table and examined the case exterior, then opened it.

The Headcount sculpture was exactly what Lace had described and what the brief had said it was: ceramic composite, half a meter on the long axis, high-gloss dark glaze, the surface worked with the almost-typographic markings running in repeating bands along the length. Under the safe house's clean work lighting it had a different presence than it would have had under the collector's market exhibition lights — less dramatic, more factual. The way objects looked when nobody was trying to sell them.

Vox examined the south face. The reading node housing at the base: a port cluster in the standard provenance documentation format, recessed into the ceramic by a half-centimeter, protected by a dust cap that Vox removed with a thumbnail and set on the table. The port was clean. Good maintenance. Someone had cared for this object.

"The data architecture," Vox said, setting up her threading cable, "is going to tell us whether this is a flat file or an active schema. The provenance chain referenced a civic registry format, but that's the outer wrapper. What it wraps determines the retrieval protocol."

"Translation," Lace said. She was leaning against the secondary workstation with her arms crossed, watching.

"Either this is a document or it's a database." Vox connected the threading interface to the port. "A document you read once. A database you query."

The connection handshake took four seconds. The workstation array shifted: two of the four channels condensed and the freed bandwidth redirected to the active query interface. The data layer began to resolve.

Rook watched Vox work.

This was Vox's particular quality: she processed information at the speed the information came in, without the visible hesitation that characterized slower analysts or the performed urgency that characterized ones who understood they were being observed. She was reading the data layer as it opened, her attention moving across the interface display with the same systematic pattern Rook had seen in her workspace — not linear, not random, the logic of it invisible from the outside and clearly present in its results.

The data layer resolved.

Vox was quiet for eleven seconds. This was not a normal length of quiet for Vox during an active data operation.

"Fifteen years," she said.

"Of what," Rook said.

"Migration patterns. Residency histories. Debt ledger cross-references. Heritage documentation filings." Vox's voice was level. She had arrived at level through the work, the same way she always did. "The GLMZ Gray Zone. All twelve zones, plus the buffer zones. Population movement, integration rates, financial dependency structures, ethnographic heritage cross-sections mapped to residency registration." She paused. "The heritage data is the significant category. It's coded to the civic grid's legacy classification schema — the framework used before the Meridian Charter harmonized the identity system in 2208. That schema hasn't been used for official civic purposes in eighteen years."

The room was quiet.

"Who would use it now," Ekow said. It was not a question.

Vox turned to look at him. "Organizations that maintained parallel heritage tracking systems when the civic schema was retired. The civic grid stopped, but the data schema is not proprietary — any institution with sufficient technical capacity could build their own registry against it." She looked back at the workstation. "I can count the organizations in the Gray Zone with that capacity on both hands. The Lotus Syndicate is one of them."

The silence extended.

"The anonymous client," Boiler said.

"Yes," Rook said.

Scout was sitting on the secondary workstation's edge, legs crossed, watching the ceiling at a particular angle that indicated she was processing rather than observing. "The Lotus commissioned a recovery job," she said, "to recover a data sculpture containing fifteen years of Gray Zone population data encoded to the heritage classification schema they use for their own registry work." A pause. "They're recovering their own data."

"Or someone stole it from them," Lace said. "And they want it back."

"Tran-Okonkwo bought it from a secondary courier," Ekow said. "He didn't know what it was — he knew it was worth more than market, but he didn't know the specific value. Which means whoever stole it from the original courier didn't know what it was either, or they would not have sold it for secondary market prices."

Rook was running the chain backward. The sculpture had appeared at the Z6 secondary market first, then at a Z4 holding facility, then at Tran-Okonkwo's market. Each move: someone who didn't know what they had, selling it to someone who also didn't know, the value accumulating in the object without anyone in the chain understanding why. Until Tran-Okonkwo had looked at it long enough to understand it was worth waiting for. And the anonymous client — the Φ12,000 anonymous client — had placed two recovery attempts before this one. Two failures in three months. The urgency was not market pressure. The urgency was operational.

"Headcount," Vox said. "The title."

Rook looked at her.

"It's a count," Vox said. "Fifteen years of counting heads. Migration patterns are a count. Debt distributions are a count. Heritage cross-sections are a count." She closed two of the query interfaces and left the heritage data layer open on the main display. "This is a census. Not an official one. A parallel census, running the same categories the official civic system retired in 2208, maintained by someone who needed the data those categories produce."

The display showed the heritage cross-section query in its schematic form: Z6, the buffer zones, every registered residency filing from the last fifteen years, matched against the legacy heritage classification framework. The distribution patterns were visible even in schematic — the concentration maps, the migration flows, the residency density by heritage classification across the Gray Zone's geography.

Data woven through the civic grid's systems so quietly, for so long, that it had been dressed as art. Somebody had been patient. Fifteen years of patience had produced this.

Rook thought about the anonymous client. Twelve thousand Quanta. Three attempts. Urgent enough to use an outside operator rather than an inside resource. She thought about what an organization with fifteen years of this data and an urgent need to recover it was planning to do with it.

She thought about Ekow's particular silence when she had named the schema format at the transit station. The weight behind the pause that had resolved into briefing language without naming what the weight was.

"Vox," she said. "Can you copy the data layer?"

"Already done," Vox said. "First action on connection. The copy is on isolated storage — not on any networked system." She looked at Rook. "I assumed we'd want options."

"We do," Rook said.

She looked at the transport case. The sculpture in its foam cradle. Φ12,000 worth of anonymous client's property, already copied, data layer opened. The contract with Ferris specified physical piece, intact, to a Z3 drop point. The contract did not say anything about the data layer being examined before delivery. The contract did not specify what happened if examining the data layer changed the calculus of the delivery.

"We're not delivering it tonight," she said.

Nobody argued.

"Ferris will need an explanation," Lace said.

"Ferris will get an explanation when I have one worth giving." Rook looked at the display. The heritage cross-section maps. The fifteen years of the Gray Zone's people, counted and categorized and held in ceramic composite dressed as art. "What else does the data connect to? What would you specifically need this data for?"

Vox was already running the query. "The heritage cross-sections are the key element. Migration patterns alone are civic planning data — the Gray Zone civic authority uses that type routinely. Debt distributions are financial profiling, standard market intelligence. But heritage mapping at this resolution, maintained against a retired classification schema for fifteen years..." She paused. "You'd need this for targeting. Population segmentation. Knowing where specific heritage classifications are concentrated and how they move and what they owe."

The room held the shape of what that meant for a moment.

Boiler said it out loud. "That's not a census. That's a target list."

"Yes," Rook said.

She looked at the copied data on isolated storage, the original sculpture in its case, the twelve thousand Quanta sitting in the contract account, and the anonymous client's drop coordinates waiting in a file she was going to leave unopened tonight.

The twelve thousand had been the point for approximately four hours. Then Vox had opened the data layer, and the twelve thousand had stopped being the point, and what had become the point instead was the question of who was building a target list over fifteen years and what they were planning to do with it now that it was complete.

That question did not have a drop point. That question required a different kind of approach entirely.

She took the sculpture from its cradle, turned it in her hands once — the worked surface, the almost-language markings, the ceramic weight of it — and set it carefully back. It was evidence now. It was also, she was beginning to understand, only one of several components in a plan that was not finished.

"We sit on the sculpture," she said. "Vox stays on the data. Tomorrow we find out who built this — and what they need to complete it."

She looked at Ekow. He was looking at the display. The heritage cross-sections. The fifteen years.

He said nothing. His right hand was very still at his side.

## Adaeze Nnodu-Park

[Beat 11]
Adaeze Nnodu-Park's office was exactly the kind of room that twelve years of fighting produced: functional, dense with the specific accumulation of ongoing work, papers in categorized stacks on the secondary desk and civic filing architecture on the primary system and the particular smell of a room continuously occupied by someone who did not stop. A community legal cooperative that had operated for twelve years had a certain material reality. The office wore it — the evidence of cases filed and cases lost and cases that had produced a record even when they produced nothing else.

She was behind the primary desk when they arrived. Early fifties, large frame, the specific deliberateness of a person who had learned to move through contested spaces without performing the contestation. She looked at Ekow first, Rook second.

"You read the data," Adaeze said, to Rook.

"Yes."

"Then you know what Headcount is."

"We know what it contains. We don't yet know what it connects to."

She nodded once. The nod acknowledged the gap and moved past it. "What I'm about to tell you fills the gap. It will make what you've seen easier to understand and harder to walk away from."

She did not wait to see whether Rook would object to the framing. She began.

Lee Nari had been a Lotus Stem adjutant for seven years. Not a Stem herself — adjutants were administrative and operational support for the council members who held the voting seats. The distinction mattered: adjutants were inside the room for most operational briefings, privy to most operational decisions, but they were not bloodline members and they did not vote. They were trusted because they were useful, and they were useful because they understood the value of the access they had been given and treated it accordingly.

Nari had been cast out at twenty-eight. The grounds were personal: she had married outside the Lotus bloodline eligibility requirements. Her husband was Emre Farhat-Adeyemi. The marriage had not been approved, had not been permitted, had in fact been explicitly forbidden under the Lotus membership rules governing bloodline adjutancy positions. She had done it anyway. The Stems had voted on her status six weeks after she filed the civil registry notice, and the vote had not been close.

She had been present in the room for a different vote three months before the vote on her own status.

This was the part Adaeze delivered without editorial. She described it as it was: Nari had been present, in her adjutancy capacity, during a Stems closed session. The session had not been unusual in format. What was unusual was the subject. The Stems had been presented with a document the Root had prepared and were asked to authorize it. The document was called, within the internal Lotus registry, the Purification Protocol.

The vote had been six-to-two in favor of authorization.

Rook heard the name. It sat in the room alongside the target list in the sculpture, alongside the heritage cross-section maps, alongside Ekow's particular silence. The three things had a shape together that they had not had separately.

She said, "Yes." She said it before Adaeze had moved to the next sentence. She said it because the pattern was already complete — the anonymous client, the fifteen years of demographic data, the unauthorized vote on a document called the Purification Protocol — and the next sentence was confirmatory information she already had the shape of. Adaeze was not surprised. She had spent twelve years fighting through institutional channels for people who could not wait for complete information. She recognized what it looked like when someone made the call early.

She continued anyway. There was more worth having.

Nari had not been permitted to take copies of documents from closed sessions. She had taken a copy of the Protocol anyway, kept it for seven years in Z6, and waited to determine what she was going to do with it and when. The timeline had now compressed. The Lotus had identified her location. The Thorns' operational pattern indicated a contact team would reach her residential address within twelve hours. Nari had filed a disclosure request with the GLMZ Civic Bureau six days ago. The Civic Bureau had not responded.

Adaeze stopped speaking.

The building was quiet around the office in the way that buffer zone buildings were quiet in the afternoon — not silent, the sounds of community work coming through the floors, the renovation Boiler was running on the east wing, the legal consultation in the room two doors down. The faint percussion of structural work overhead: load-bearing interventions, the specific sound of someone doing something that was meant to last.

"The Purification Protocol," Rook said. "What specifically."

"Nari can explain what the document contained," Adaeze said. "I have her summary. The Protocol is a systematic population management initiative targeting non-eligible heritage classifications in the Z6 buffer zone. Phased displacement over a twenty-year timeline. Three operational components required for Phase 1 deployment."

Rook waited.

"A demographic baseline," Adaeze said. "Fifteen years of heritage cross-section data."

She did not say what Rook was already thinking. She went on.

"Neuretics registration infrastructure access. A way to identify and log every registered neuretics user in the Z6 network by heritage classification, in real time. And a deployment window — thirty days from the moment the first two components are in place." She looked at Rook directly. "Nari's summary describes those requirements from the document she read seven years ago. The document she has is the authorization record. It has the Stems' signatures."

Ekow had not spoken since they had come in. He was standing near the office door — the position he took in every room. He had been watching Adaeze's face while she spoke and now he was not watching anything, his eyes on a middle distance that had nothing in it.

"Twelve hours from the Thorn contact schedule," he said.

"Yes," Adaeze said.

"You confirmed this from the pattern analysis or from inside the schedule."

She paused fractionally. "Pattern analysis. I don't have inside access to the Thorn operational queue."

He nodded. The nod was not about her answer. He was verifying something he already knew, running the operational logic he had been inside against what she was telling him from the outside of it. His hands rested at his sides.

"The Thorns use a three-stage contact protocol for a casting-out extraction," he said. Not to Adaeze. To the room. "Surveillance confirmation of current address, which they will have completed before the clock starts. Then a route feasibility assessment — they map the extraction path from her building and identify alternates. That takes four to six hours. Then contact deployment: a two-person entry team, assigned before the feasibility assessment is complete." He paused. "If the pattern analysis read the surveillance confirmation as the start of the twelve-hour window, we're at the end of the route assessment stage. Possibly into early contact deployment."

The room was quieter.

Adaeze looked at him. Her expression had not changed significantly, but something in her attention had sharpened — not surprise, exactly. The update of a person encountering more specific information than she had expected from the source. "That's a more precise timeline than I had," she said.

"Yes," Ekow said. He said it without elaboration and without apology.

Rook was watching his face. He met Adaeze's gaze with the same stillness he brought to everything — the practiced containment of a man who had sat in rooms where this kind of decision was made and had listened and had known the operational standard from the inside and had not been in a position to use that knowledge until now. She was aware, at the edge of her operational thinking, of what it meant that he could describe the Thorns' three-stage contact protocol with this specificity. She filed it. She would develop it later.

"Where is Nari now," Rook said.

"Her Z6 residential address. She hasn't moved since I spoke with her this morning — moving independently draws Thorn attention faster than holding position. She has a route I outlined but she doesn't use it unless I confirm."

"Has the route been walked?"

"Once, by me, three days ago." A pause. "I'm not an operator."

"I know." Rook looked at the desk. The civic filing architecture on the primary system. The categorized stacks on the secondary desk. Twelve years of fighting Lotus debt structures through institutional channels and two Civic Bureau petitions that had gone nowhere and had been filed anyway because filing the record was not the same as expecting the Bureau to act. The cooperative had survived. Adaeze was still here. She had called in something extra-institutional today, which meant the institutional channels had run as far as they were going to run.

"What does Nari know about the Protocol beyond the authorization vote?" Rook said.

"She knows what the document contained. She knows who authorized it. She knows the operational timeline as it existed seven years ago, which may have advanced significantly since." Adaeze held her gaze. "She waited fifteen years to use this. She will tell you what you need to know when she trusts you enough to tell you."

"She'll trust us enough when we've gotten her clear," Rook said.

"Yes," Adaeze said.

Rook looked at Ekow again. He was still at the door. The expression she had not been able to read precisely for most of the meeting had settled into something she could now place: not resolution, not readiness, something more like the moment before a man does something he decided on a long time ago and has simply been waiting for the conditions to be right.

"Ekow," she said. "The south approach to Nari's building. Tell me what you know."

He looked at her directly. "I've used it," he said. "I know where the Thorn coverage has gaps and I know the route that avoids the staging facility west of her block." He paused. "I know which maintenance access connects to the crawlway network on her floor. I used that access four years ago. The building infrastructure hasn't changed."

The room absorbed this.

"We'll need current Thorn positions in Z6," Rook said. Not to Ekow specifically — to the team, to Vox on the channel. "The extraction route walked again before we move. And I want to know what the twelve-hour window looks like from the route feasibility stage Ekow just described." She looked at Adaeze. "Tell Nari we're coming. Don't give her a time. Tell her tonight."

Adaeze nodded. The nod was the one she used when she had been doing the work that could be done with existing resources and a new resource had just been added and the calculation had shifted from uncertain to possible. It was a careful nod. She was not a person who performed relief.

"Tonight," she said.

Outside in the corridor, the sound of Boiler's renovation work continued overhead: the rhythmic percussion of structural intervention in progress, someone determining what held and what released under pressure, someone doing the work of making a thing that would last.

[Beat 12]
They moved the briefing to the safe house. Adaeze's building had too much foot traffic for an operational brief and not enough secure communications infrastructure for what Ekow needed to run. They drove in two vehicles, staggered as before. The team channel went quiet during the transit, which was the channel's way of indicating there was nothing immediate to say and the silence was not a gap.

Ekow had a physical map.

Not standard — Rook had expected him to pull routing data from neuretics mesh or request Vox's threading architecture. Instead he produced a folded physical print, Z6 grid overlay, the kind of print the civic mapping office generated for infrastructure maintenance purposes and that lived in the permit archives. It was not the current revision. The date stamp in the lower corner was eighteen months prior. He had annotated it by hand: small, precise notations in blue marking compound, the kind of permanent marking that did not smear and did not reflect under standard illumination. The annotation layer was dense. Significant work had gone into this map over a significant period of time.

He spread it on the safe house's cleared table. The transport case had been moved to the storage room. The workstation was running Vox's isolated copy of the Headcount data. The map occupied the center of the cleared surface.

"Z6 and the buffer south," he said, placing his hands at two points on the map to establish the geography. His hands were large and still on the page, the reinforced bone structure of his combat frame visible in the way they rested — not weight, exactly, but density. "Nari's residential block is here, Z6 eastern sector, four blocks from the buffer boundary." His finger moved east, then traced a corridor. "The Thorn primary patrol covers this — the arterial and the two cross-streets that frame it. The patrol cycle runs on a forty-minute rotation in the evening, dropping to a twenty-five-minute rotation between twenty-two hundred and zero two hundred when the pedestrian density drops and the activity window opens."

Rook looked at the map. The annotations: blue marks at regular intervals she read, after a moment, as surveillance node positions. Blue brackets around three blocks she read as safe-house designations. Small asterisks at five points she could not immediately interpret.

"The asterisks," she said.

"Surveillance dead zones. Locations where the Thorn coverage overlap fails — either the node placement doesn't account for the building geometry, or the coverage frequency is lower than the surrounding area because of the node's maintenance history. If you're moving through Z6 and you don't want to be logged, those are the positions where the gap is reliable."

"You mapped these," Vox said. She was at the workstation, half her attention on the Headcount query and half on the briefing.

"Over time," Ekow said. He did not elaborate.

Boiler was looking at the map the way he looked at structural drawings: methodically, the attention moving in the systematic pattern of someone reading load paths rather than geography. He put his finger on the blue bracket marking the closest Lotus safe house to Nari's building. "This one's active?"

"As of four days ago. It runs as a debt collection administrative office during business hours. After twenty-one hundred it's a staging facility. Standard two-person occupancy for contact deployment work."

"You know the rotation for that facility specifically," Boiler said.

"Yes."

Boiler looked up from the map. He and Ekow looked at each other for a moment. Boiler's expression was the careful openness of a person holding a question he had already answered and was deciding whether the answer needed to be spoken. He decided it didn't. He looked back at the map.

Scout was at the workstation edge, watching the map rather than the screen. "The maintenance crawlway network under the Z6 eastern sector," she said. "Is it still running the pre-2218 civic layout?"

"Yes. The Z6 infrastructure budget hasn't funded a layout update since 2214. The crawlways are the original 2180s installation with patched sections." He moved to the map's lower margin, where a second annotation layer in slightly different notation ran parallel to the street grid. "The original layout is here. I've marked the patched sections — those run different access parameters than the original installation. Scout's threading equipment will handle both, but the access sequence differs between original and patched hardware."

Scout was already noting something in her mesh.

Lace had been quiet since they had entered the safe house. She was standing at the periphery of the briefing space, not quite inside the map's orbit, with the expression she wore when she was running an internal assessment she had not yet completed. She looked at the annotations on the map — the years of precise marking in blue compound, the surveillance dead zones and patrol rotations and safe-house designations — and her assessment resolved into something she was going to say.

"How long have you had this map," she said.

"Six years," Ekow said. "Continuously updated."

"Six years of updating an operational map of the Lotus Z6 enforcement infrastructure." She said it without inflection, the same way she said other factual statements. "You kept this current while you were running contract jobs for them."

"Yes," he said.

The room was quiet.

Rook was watching Ekow's face. He met Lace's gaze without the deflection of a man being accused and without the performance of a man being exonerated. He met it with the stillness of someone who had waited for this moment for a long time and had decided how he was going to inhabit it when it arrived. His face said: I kept this map. I know what the map contains and what it means that I have it. I am not asking you to resolve that for me. That is mine to carry.

His right hand was very still at his side.

Lace held his gaze for three seconds. Then she looked back at the map. "The dead zone marked at the northeast corner of Nari's block," she said. "How large is the coverage gap?"

"Reliable for approximately eight minutes before the sweep cycle corrects," Ekow said. "If you need more than eight minutes at that position, you'll need to be through it before the correction, not waiting for a second gap."

"Eight minutes is enough for a building entry," Scout said.

"With the right threading sequence, yes." He moved back to the map and pointed to the position. "The building has a maintenance access on the north face that connects to the crawlway junction here. That junction is the original 2180s installation — Scout can thread it with standard access protocol. If the contact team is coming from the Thorn staging facility, they'll approach from the west on the arterial. That approach takes them away from this access point entirely. You have a separate ingress from their ingress."

"How long from the staging facility to her building on their standard route," Rook said.

"Nine minutes walking. Fourteen if they run surveillance protocol rather than direct approach."

"They'll run surveillance protocol," she said.

"Yes." A pause. "The contact teams always run surveillance protocol for casting-out extractions. It's in the operational standard. They don't approach directly — they confirm position and route first." He said this without the emphasis that would have made it something other than a factual briefing. He had read the Thorns' operational standard. He had been adjacent to it for twelve years.

Vox had turned her full attention to the briefing now, the Headcount query running in secondary while she listened. She was cataloguing, Rook could tell — not just the operational data but the source structure, the way the information was organized, what it revealed about how and when it had been acquired.

"The extraction window," Rook said. "Best timing given the patrol cycle."

"Between twenty-two forty-five and zero one hundred," Ekow said. "The gap in the secondary patrol rotation — the point where the patrol teams overlap at the west end of the arterial, creating a coverage gap in the eastern sector. The gap runs approximately forty minutes. If you're moving Nari from her building to the buffer boundary, you want to enter the buffer in that window." He looked at Rook. "I can walk the extraction route. I know where the Thorns will be."

The room absorbed this.

He knew the patrol rotations, the surveillance dead zones, the staging facility occupancy, the extraction route, the contact team's operational standard. He had been inside this for twelve years. He had kept a current map and had updated it continuously while running jobs that required this knowledge, which meant the knowledge had been acquired and maintained as something separate from the job requirements. The jobs didn't need a continuously updated map of surveillance dead zones. Whatever had needed that map was something he had been carrying in a private register for six years.

Nobody said this.

Boiler looked in the direction of the community center's east wing through the safe house wall — through the building, toward the place where he had been doing renovation work for weeks. His expression was the expression of a man running calculations, the same one he wore on the scaffold board.

"The staging facility at the west end of the buffer," he said. "The one marked with a double bracket."

"A community health clinic during the day," Ekow said. "Evening, it runs a debt ledger management operation. Standard Lotus dual-function facility."

Boiler nodded slowly. He looked at the map and he did not say what he was thinking, which was the same thing everyone else in the room was thinking. The debt collectors in the clinic building, at the same address as the health service. The surveillance facility in the administrative office. The dual function — the simultaneous service and the simultaneous mechanism, the same institution wearing two faces at different hours without contradiction.

He said, instead: "The load paths in that sector are going to be complicated."

"Yes," Ekow said. And his voice had, for the first time in the briefing, a particular quality — not regret, not apology, something more structural than either. The acknowledgment of a man who had spent twelve years as part of the load-bearing infrastructure of something that also happened to be this. It was not a confession. It was a reckoning conducted quietly, in front of people who had seen enough to understand it, and it was over in a sentence.

Rook filed the quality and let it sit.

"Tonight," she said. "We need the extraction route walked before twenty-two hundred. Ekow, you take Scout on the route confirmation. I want current surveillance positions verified against the map, and I want to know which dead zones are still reliable at the hour we'll be using them."

"Yes," Ekow said.

He folded the map with the care of a man who had maintained an object for six years and was not done needing it. His hands were precise with it. He slid it back into the inside pocket of his field gear, and the gesture had the quality of a thing being carried that was also, in this company, being shared for the first time — not handed over, not surrendered, but no longer carried alone.

"Two hours," Scout said, checking her threading queue. "I'll have the crawlway access sequences loaded before we walk the route."

"Good," Rook said. "Vox, I still need the anonymous client identity. Midnight or earlier."

"Working," Vox said, from the workstation. Four channels, the streams running. She was already in it.

The safe house organized itself around the operational picture. This was what it looked like when a crew moved from the briefing stage to the working stage — the quality of the air changed, the spaces between people contracted slightly, the silence became the silence of people doing things rather than people considering things. Rook had been in enough rooms at this stage to know it by feel.

She looked at the map's impression in the air over the cleared table, where the paper had been.

Six years of private accounting. Now this room's accounting. The same load. Different shoulders carrying it.

[Beat 13]
Adaeze had been explaining the Civic Bureau's non-response when Rook said, "Yes."

This was afterward, she understood, not during. She was reconstructing the sequence of her agreement from the position of having already made it, the way you reconstructed a step you had taken without fully deciding to take it. Adaeze had been three-quarters through the brief. The Civic Bureau had filed nothing, responded to nothing, treated Nari's disclosure request as a document in a queue. The Thorns had a contact schedule. The twelve-hour window. The document with six signatures.

She had said yes before Adaeze reached the part about what specifically she was asking the crew to do.

Adaeze had paused when she said it. Not from surprise — from the professional recognition that the calculation had completed before the briefing was complete. She had continued anyway. The remaining information was worth having. Rook had listened to the rest of it from the position of someone who had already agreed and was now learning the specific shape of what they had agreed to.

The rest of the brief had confirmed what she had already agreed to.

She was not surprised that she had agreed. She noted this while she was at the safe house table an hour later, with Ekow's annotated map under her hands and Vox's four channels running the Headcount data query and the crew distributed around the cleared space in the positions they occupied when they were operational rather than social. The positions said: we are in this now.

She was not surprised. She was a little surprised she was not surprised.

---

Two threads.

The sculpture was in the storage room, data layer copied, physical piece held. The anonymous client's drop coordinates sat unopened in her neuretics queue — she had checked them twice, confirmed the format, decided not to read the full coordinates until she had made the formal decision about delivery. She had made it: they weren't delivering to the anonymous client, not yet, not until she knew who the anonymous client was. The sculpture was Thread 1. Hold the asset, identify the source, decide what to do with the information.

The extraction was Thread 2. Get Nari clear before the Thorn contact team completed their route assessment. Get the document with her. Decide what to do with that information also.

Two threads. The operational problem was not whether to run them simultaneously — the twelve-hour window was not a negotiable parameter. The operational problem was resource allocation: where each crew member needed to be, how the threads communicated, what happened if one thread developed complications while the other was committed.

She walked through it out loud. Ekow on the extraction route — the Z6 knowledge, the south approach, the maintenance access he had used himself four years ago. Boiler as physical backup in Z6 — the community geography he had been watching for six months from a scaffold board, the buffer zone blocks he could navigate without a map. Scout threading the crawlways and the coverage network, two independent paths into the building's systems. Lace in reserve for the soft-cover layer if the contact team's approach complicated the extraction exit — the right face in the right hallway at the right moment, the kind of problem Lace solved by being present rather than by doing anything in particular. Vox on both threads simultaneously, the data analysis on Thread 1 running parallel to the intelligence overlay on Thread 2. Rook across them.

Six people, two jobs, one source they did not yet know they shared.

"The sculpture client," Lace said. She was looking at the ceiling in the particular way she looked at things she was reassembling in her mind. "Anonymous. Twelve thousand. Three attempts before us. They waited long enough for the piece to move twice." A pause. "They knew the piece existed and they knew it was in the secondary market. They didn't know exactly where it was for three months. That's an unusual gap for an organization that wants something badly enough to pay twelve thousand."

"They lost the chain of custody when the original courier went to ground," Vox said. "The piece entered the gray market without their knowledge. They were tracking it through the secondary market feeds, which have the inherent delay of public channels."

"Why would an organization with the resources to commission a twelve-thousand Quanta recovery not have direct intelligence on the Z6 secondary market," Lace said. It was not a question she was directing at anyone specific. It was a question she was directing at the shape of the situation.

Ekow was quiet.

"A legitimate organization wouldn't," Scout said. "One with official market presence would have current feeds on Z4/Z6 secondary activity. The delay argues for an organization that maintains unofficial market access, which means the intelligence comes through secondary sources with inherent lag."

"Or an organization that maintains a public face as a legitimate institution and runs its unofficial operations through separate channels that don't share intelligence across the boundary," Lace said.

She said this without looking at Ekow. Nobody looked at Ekow.

The room did the calculation in silence, which was the efficient way to do it. The anonymous client for the Headcount sculpture: Φ12,000, three attempts, fifteen years of Gray Zone demographic data in heritage classification schema. Ekow's particular quiet when she had first named the schema format at the transit station. Adaeze's briefing: the Purification Protocol's operational requirements — a demographic baseline as the first component.

The two threads shared a source. They did not yet know this as a confirmed fact — not named, not placed on the operational board as established intelligence. But the shape of it was visible in the way the shape of a load-bearing problem was visible in a building: not because you could see the stress, but because you could see where the structure had been compensating for it.

"We need the anonymous client's identity before we can make the connection explicit," Rook said.

"I'm working on it," Vox said. "The contract wall through Ferris is solid. There's an indirect approach through the Z3 drop coordinates — whoever set up the drop point used a commercial staging address, and commercial staging addresses have lease records. It takes time."

"How much time."

"Half a day. Maybe less if the lease trail is shallow."

She nodded. Half a day was enough time to complete the extraction if the extraction ran cleanly, and if they had the client's identity by the time Nari was clear, they would have both threads in hand simultaneously. The question of what to do with two threads, both of which led to the same source, was a question for after they had confirmed the source. One problem at a time.

"If we're right about the connection," Boiler said, carefully, "then the anonymous client has an operational interest in both what we're holding and in keeping Nari from talking."

"Yes," Rook said.

He absorbed this. "Which makes this not two jobs with one source. This makes us actively working against our own client on Thread 2."

"We took the job before we knew what the job was," she said.

"I know." He was not accusing her of anything. He was noting the position they were in — the professional accuracy of a man who understood that load-bearing structures needed to be assessed correctly even when the assessment was uncomfortable. "Which means we're in the position of having been paid to work against the people we're now working against."

"Half of the contract," she said. "The second tranche isn't paid until we deliver the physical piece. We're not going to deliver."

Boiler looked at the ceiling for a moment, then back down. "All right," he said — Boiler's way of indicating he had run the calculation and arrived at the answer and had accepted the answer without requiring the answer to be anything other than what it was.

Lace unfolded her arms. "The twelve thousand is gone."

"The twelve thousand was never the point," Rook said.

"I know," Lace said. "I'm noting it for the record." A pause. "What does the anonymous client do when we don't deliver?"

"They go to Ferris," Rook said. "Ferris tells them the delivery is delayed. We have enough buffer in the contract language for that hold. They won't pressure Ferris immediately — they'll wait, and while they're waiting, the extraction completes and Nari's documentation starts moving through the disclosure pipeline."

"And if they connect us to the extraction before Nari's documentation is filed."

"They might," Rook said. "We'll be quicker."

Lace held this for a moment. Then she nodded — not Boiler's structural all right, but something closer to Lace's own register: the acknowledgment of a person who had spent years inhabiting other people's positions and had found, in this one, a reason to inhabit her own.

Rook looked around the table. The crew in their operational positions. Six people who had not been in the same room together three weeks ago, now arranged around a cleared safe house table in the specific formation of a crew that had already committed.

She was aware, again, of the moment she had said yes at Adaeze's desk. Before the brief was complete. Before the full operational picture was available. She had done this before — taken jobs before all the information was in, worked forward into the shape of things rather than waiting for the shape to resolve before committing. She understood this as a professional habit and also as something that had been calibrating, in the last three months, toward different objects. The count that held names now. The Reibo flechette she had not put down.

She had agreed before Adaeze finished. She was not surprised she had agreed. She was a little surprised she was not surprised, and she was not sure yet what the absence of that surprise was pointing at.

She let it sit. There was work to do.

"Two threads," she said. "We start the extraction approach at twenty-two hundred. Vox, I need the anonymous client identity by midnight or earlier. Ekow, Scout — walk the extraction route once more before twenty-two hundred. Current surveillance positions verified."

"Working," Vox said.

"Yes," Ekow said.

"Lace," Rook said.

She looked up from wherever she had been.

"When we know who the client is," Rook said, "you'll know what face to be when we meet them."

Lace smiled briefly — the small authentic version, the one she didn't put on for anyone. "I'll know before you do," she said.

"Yes," Rook said. "You probably will."

## Old Harbor

[Beat 14]
The free clinic ran until midnight. Rook noted this because they were walking past it at twenty-one forty and the door was still lit, the interior visible through the front glass: a waiting area with six people in it, two staff in pale green uniforms behind the reception counter, the standard civic health facility layout adapted for a Z6/buffer building — lower-ceiling intake rooms, more portable equipment than the Z3 version of the same service, the specific economies of infrastructure built for people who were not the GLMZ's primary investment priority.

The sign above the door was the Lotus standard community health identifier. Not the Syndicate's name, which did not appear on any of its institutional infrastructure, but the stylized green-and-white mark that Z6 residents read as health service the way they read the civic water mark as water: immediately, without reading the name behind it.

"Third community clinic in three blocks," Ekow said. He was walking beside her, the other crew distributed behind and across the street in the standard coverage geometry of people who knew each other's spatial habits. "This district has the highest clinic density in Z6. The Lotus established the first in 2191. The second in 2197. The third one in 2208."

"The same year the Meridian Charter harmonized the civic identity system," Vox said, in the team channel, from her Z4 workspace. Her voice was factual, not editorial.

"Yes," Ekow said. "When the civic heritage classification schema was retired. The year the official census stopped counting what the Lotus was counting." He kept walking. "The clinic expansion was not coincidental."

The street was Z6 evening — not late enough for the full quiet, still populated enough for the pedestrian rhythm of an active community with a specific relationship to its neighborhood hours. People who lived here. People who knew the street. A density of recognition, of neighbors and small transactions and the ambient community texture of a place that had been the same place for long enough to have layers.

The debt collection office was four doors from the clinic. The sign read Lotus Community Financial Services and the design language was the same: green-and-white identifier, the institutional face that said GLMZ service provider, civic-standard, available to residents. The posted hours were nine to seventeen. The light inside was still on.

"Evening hours," Scout said, from across the street.

"The administrative hours are public record," Ekow said. "The operational hours are different. Debt ledger work runs evenings — it's when the collections teams come in for route assignments and the week's outstanding accounts are processed." He paused at the intersection and oriented the group on the next block before continuing. "The health workers and the collections staff enter through the same door. Some of them know each other. Some of them have been both at different points in their careers."

Boiler was walking slightly behind Rook, and she heard him exhale — not a sigh, the structural sound of a man running a calculation that had just confirmed an answer he had held open until confirmation. Something that had been possibility settling into weight.

"Same institutional mark," Lace said. She was not asking.

"No distinction of affiliation," Ekow said. "The clinic and the collections office are the same organization with different service designations. The staff distinguish by function rather than identity. Most of the people who use the clinic do not connect it to the same entity that manages their debt ledger." He paused. "Some do. They use the clinic anyway. There is no alternative clinic."

Rook walked past the collections office and thought about what it meant to live in a neighborhood where the institution that took care of you and the institution that collected from you were the same institution. Not presented as the same — the signs were distinct, the service hours distinct, the language of each interaction separate. But traceable to the same source if you looked at the right records, if you knew which names appeared on both incorporation documents.

Most people did not look at the right records. Most people experienced the services separately, which was the design of it. The design was what made it work.

The market was a block east. They walked through it.

It was a night market — the distinction was in the hours and the product category, the night market running repair services and prepared food and the kind of commerce that the daytime economy pushed to the margins. The stalls were fixed infrastructure, not portable, which meant the market had been operating at this address long enough to warrant the investment in permanent fixtures. Three years minimum for the build-out. More likely five or more.

The stalls ran on the ground floor of a mixed-use building, the upper floors residential. The market had its own specific quality — not the Z3 collector market's careful curation, not the Z4 commercial density. It was a market that served a community that knew it, the product knowledge distributed, the transactions familiar, the vendors and buyers in the ongoing relationship of a regular exchange.
