A woman with two children was negotiating over repair tools at one stall. An older man was collecting a food order from a window three stalls down. The density was functional. It was also, in the specific texture of it, real.

Boiler had stopped moving.

He was standing at the market's edge, at the gap between the first row of stalls and the building footprint, looking up at the structural interface between the market infrastructure and the residential above. His expression was the assessment expression — not professional neutrality but the focused attention of a person reading a load path.

"The market column foundations are integrated into the residential building's footprint," he said. "They're not separate structures. The market's infrastructure is load-bearing for the residential above."

"Yes," Ekow said. He was watching Boiler with the careful attention of someone who understood why this observation mattered without needing to be told. "The Lotus built the market in 2204. The residential above was built in 2198 by the community housing cooperative. The market foundation integration happened during a renovation in 2209. The cooperative authorized it."

"They authorized it because the market provided structural support," Boiler said. He was not asking. "And maintenance funding. And because the cooperative was, by 2209, two years into a debt structure with the Lotus financial services operation."

"Yes," Ekow said.

Boiler looked at the building for another moment — the way the original residential poured concrete met the later market column integration, the junction visible in the slightly different aggregate texture if you knew what you were looking at. Then he resumed walking. He did not say what he was thinking. He did not need to.

Rook was thinking it anyway. The debt structure that funded the building renovation also integrated the market infrastructure into the residential foundation, which meant the building now had a load-bearing dependency on the market, which meant the people living above could not be housed without the market below, which meant the Lotus was load-bearing for the people it was also collecting from. You could not remove one without removing the other. You could not serve these people without the mechanism and you could not stop the mechanism without failing the service.

This was the mechanism. Not the service-as-cover and the exploitation-as-reality running in separate registers. They were the same structure. They had been built simultaneously as one structure.

She thought about what Boiler had said on the scaffold board two weeks ago, about the difference between what holds and what performs holding, about identifying the failure mode and eliminating it while preserving the structure. The renovation problem and the Lotus problem were not analogous. They were the same problem at different scales.

"The Lotus has been the load-bearing element for twenty years," she said. Not to anyone in particular.

Boiler heard it. He looked at her sideways without stopping. "Yes," he said. "That's what I've been trying to figure out how to say."

They moved north through Old Harbor for another twenty minutes, Ekow taking them through the routes he had walked for years — the supply corridors behind the market infrastructure, the clinic service alleys, the residential blocks where the street layout had been influenced by Lotus development work over four decades until the neighborhood geography itself was organized around the Syndicate's presence. Every turn, Ekow knew. Every address, he could annotate. The map in his coat pocket was not an intelligence document. It was a record of how deeply an organization he had worked for had embedded itself in the physical structure of this place, and how the physical structure had grown to depend on that embedding.

The free clinics and the debt collectors and the community markets and the maintenance integration and the twenty years of service and leverage, all of it continuous, all of it real. The people in the waiting room at twenty-one forty were being taken care of and they were in the care of an organization that had also authorized the Purification Protocol, and those two facts were not separable, and the people in the waiting room probably did not know the second fact and would not have had a different option if they had.

This was the thing Rook kept arriving at. Not that the Syndicate was evil dressed as good — that framing was too clean, and clean framings were the kind that failed under examination. The Syndicate was good and the Syndicate was the mechanism of something terrible and those two things were not compartments, they were a single load path, and the question of what you did with that was not a question about the Lotus. It was a question about what replaced it.

At the northern boundary of the Old Harbor district, where the Z6 grid began its transition to the buffer zone, they stopped. The street ahead was quieter than the market district — residential, the hour when the evening commerce had closed and the night shift hadn't fully begun. A community asleep.

The sounds from the direction of the free clinic were still audible: the ambient quiet of a health service running its late shift.

Scout was looking back toward the market. "The crawlway junction at Nari's block connects to the same infrastructure network as the market district," she said. "The maintenance access is continuous — if we need secondary egress, we can use the market's service network."

"Good," Rook said.

"The Lotus debt management office three blocks east has a maintenance junction on the same network," Ekow said. "If they're monitoring crawlway traffic, they'll have a passive sensor on that junction."

"Can you thread around it," Rook said to Scout.

"Yes. Forty seconds to identify the sensor signature and build a bypass loop. Straightforward for original-installation hardware."

"Note it," Rook said. "Primary plan avoids it. If we need the secondary egress, you have forty seconds."

She turned north. Nari's building was two blocks ahead.

"No one lives here because they want to be controlled," Lace said. She said it quietly, not in the team channel — just to the air, to the fact of the street they were standing on. "They live here because this is where they can afford to live. And the services they use are the services available to them."

"Yes," Ekow said.

"And the services are real," she said.

"Yes," he said again. His voice had the quality it had carried the whole walk: not apology, not endorsement. The flat precision of a person stating conditions that he had been inside for twelve years and was only now finding the language for, because the language required a position outside them to construct and this was the first time he had had that position.

The walk had given them something. Not an answer to the Lotus problem — there was no answer available on this street, on this night, with this crew. But a map of the thing they were in. A topology of the load paths. What held and what released and what the people living in these buildings would need if the load paths changed.

She filed it. She would need it later.

"We move," she said. "Two blocks. Stay spread."

The crew organized itself around the north approach to Nari's building, the coverage geometry they had been practicing since the Z3 job, the positions they had each found in the group's operational logic. Ekow at the lead. Rook behind him. The others distributed across the block in the quiet and purposeful formation of people who were going somewhere, not people who were leaving somewhere.

The street was Z6 at twenty-two hundred. The city was mostly asleep. The clinic behind them ran until midnight.

[Beat 15]
Rook's knock produced movement immediately behind the door — not the movement of someone woken or caught unawares, but the immediate response of someone who had been awake and had been expecting a knock. The sequence: footsteps to the door, a pause for the security interval, the door opening.

Lee Nari was forty-three and looked it in the way that meant she had been paying attention to her years rather than managing them. Medium height, precise posture, the kind of stillness that was not tension but the long-term result of living carefully in a space where certain kinds of movement had consequences. She was wearing clothes she had slept in or expected to leave in — the category that covered both conditions without distinguishing between them.

She looked at Rook first, then Ekow. Something in her face shifted when she saw him. Not recognition in the street-recognition sense — something more specific than that, the particular attention of a person who has been carrying a thing for a long time and has just encountered someone else who carries the same kind of weight.

"Adaeze sent you," she said. Her voice was even.

"Yes," Rook said.

She stepped back from the door.

The apartment was exactly the size it needed to be and no larger. Z6 residential, single-room with a separate sleeping area, the dimensions that the buffer zone building codes produced at the lower rental tier. The furniture was functional and sufficient — a table, two chairs, a shelf unit running the west wall. On the shelf: books, physical ones, the Z6 reading economy that ran on secondhand print because not everyone in these zones had mesh access and physical books did not require registration or access fees. A small kitchen unit. One window, Z6-standard frosted security glass with the privacy film that was standard in the eastern sector.

And by the door, on the floor to the left of the entrance: a bag.

Single-strap carry, mid-size, the civilian equivalent of a field pack. It was not positioned the way a bag was positioned when someone had left it there on the way in. It was positioned the way a bag was positioned when someone had placed it there with care and knowledge and specific intent — the strap looped over the lower door handle, the bag's weight settled and ready. Everything about it said: this has been here for a while.

The bag was not packed for tonight. The bag had been packed for three years.

Rook looked at it for a moment and filed it and did not comment on it.

Nari had not looked at the bag. She had not needed to. She knew where it was — had always known, the way you knew the location of every object in a room you had maintained in a specific state for a long time.

"How long do we have," she said.

"We leave in twenty minutes," Rook said.

Nari nodded. She moved to the table and sat in the chair that faced the door. Not because she wanted to sit — from the way she sat, she had been sitting most of the day, the stillness of someone who had been waiting and had found the particular posture of waiting. The chair was oriented to the door. This had also been true for a while.

"You've read my summary," she said.

"Adaeze briefed us on the outline," Rook said. "You'll tell us the rest when you're clear."

Nari looked at her with the measured attention of a woman assessing whether "when you're clear" was a condition being offered or being prepared for her to accept. The assessment was brief and its conclusion was pragmatic. She had waited fifteen years. She was not going to argue about sequencing now.

"Yes," she said. "When I'm clear."

She looked at Ekow.

He was standing near the door, in the position he took in every room. He was looking at her with an expression Rook could not read precisely, which was unusual. She could normally read Ekow's expressions by function if not by content. This one had a quality she had not seen on him before — not quite recognition, not quite something arrived at, something in the space between.

"You know me," Nari said, to Ekow. Not a question.

"By record," he said. "Not in person."

She absorbed this without asking what the record said. Whatever the record contained, she was familiar with the kind of record it was — the kind maintained by organizations that maintained records on people they had cast out, the kind accessible to someone working in close proximity to those organizations. She knew what his presence here alongside Adaeze's referral meant. She did not require him to explain it.

"Adaeze trusts you," she said.

"I know," he said.

"That's enough," she said.

Rook gave herself a moment to read the room. The physical record of fifteen years in this apartment. The books on the shelf were organized in a pattern that said daily use rather than display — certain volumes more worn than others, the reading material of someone who had been in the same place long enough for the books to reflect seasons of attention. The window with its security film. The table with the slight lean from a floor's imperfection. A photograph on the shelf beside the books, small format, a man and a woman in a context that read as an ordinary occasion — not a celebration, something more everyday, a meal somewhere, the kind of photograph you kept because it was an ordinary Tuesday and the ordinary Tuesday had mattered.

She noted the photograph without examining it.

Nari had been following her gaze. She looked at the photograph, then away. "Emre," she said. "He died six years ago. It was ruled an accident."

She said this in the same register she had used for everything else she had said since they walked in — level, precise, carrying no additional weight in its delivery. The sentence sat in the room at the same altitude as the other sentences. Her face held the same expression it had held before she said it.

Rook heard it. She filed it. She did not respond beyond a nod of acknowledgment — not because she didn't hear what was under the flatness, but because Nari had delivered it without the weight and adding the weight back would have been a commentary on how Nari chose to carry what she carried, and that was not Rook's commentary to make.

"You have a minute to take anything from the room," Rook said. "The bag is ready."

"The bag has everything," Nari said. "I thought I'd be ready in ten minutes when I first packed it. I started earlier than that." She said this without self-consciousness, the matter-of-fact quality of someone reporting a practical fact about a decision they had made years ago and had not regretted. She stood from the chair.

Ekow said, "Rook."

She turned. He was looking at her with the expression she couldn't read.

"Give us a minute," he said.

She looked at him for a moment. Then she looked at Nari, who was looking at Ekow with an expression that was not quite recognition and not quite something else — the attention of a person who had been carrying a thing for a long time and had just recognized someone else who was carrying the same kind of thing, and was deciding whether that recognition was a reason to say something or a reason to say nothing, and was arriving at the second answer.

Rook stepped out.

She stood in the corridor, which was Z6 residential in every dimension — narrow, the utility access panels on the east wall, the corridor lighting at half-power, the specific smell of a building at twenty-two hundred when most of the residents were asleep. Scout was at the stairwell end, watching the building entry floor. Boiler was one level down. Lace was on the street level with the egress route mapped.

The door was closed.

Rook stood in the corridor and did not listen. This was not discipline — she could not have heard through a door at this quality of construction. This was the closed door given as a gift, because Ekow had asked for it and had not needed to explain why he was asking, and she had understood without needing the explanation. Nari had fifteen years of something she had been carrying in this apartment. Ekow had twelve years of something he had been carrying somewhere else. Whatever was in the room between them was not for the crew.

She found herself looking at the utility access panels on the east wall. Standard Z6 maintenance architecture, the panel housings in the original installation ferrocement, the conduit labels faded but legible. Infrastructure that had been doing the same job for forty years without announcement. Old construction. You could feel the density of it if you pressed a palm to the wall — the specific resistance of material that had been in place long enough to have settled into its load entirely.

She pressed a palm to the wall.

The door opened at six minutes. Nari came out first — the bag over her shoulder, strap adjusted, weight settled into the carry position. The movement was practiced, automatic: she had tried the bag on enough times that the adjustment required no thought. It was ready. She had been ready.

Ekow came out behind her.

His face was the face she recognized.

She did not ask what had been said. She would not ask. The closed door was what it was and she had given it, and whatever the room contained stayed in the room. She was aware that something had shifted in the set of him — not changed, exactly, but shifted, the way a load shifts when it is redistributed rather than removed. Still carrying. Carrying differently.

"We move," she said.

She keyed the team channel: "We have the package. Heading for the primary extraction route. Scout, confirm the east crawlway access."

"Confirmed," Scout said. "Entry sequence loaded. The maintenance access is clear."

"Boiler."

"Ready," he said, from below.

She looked at Nari. The bag on her shoulder, three years of readiness made portable. She had been packed before the crew had taken the job. She had been ready before the twelve-hour window had been named. She had been ready, probably, since the day she had filed the Protocol document in the category of when I die I will have done nothing and had looked at that file and decided she was not done yet.

"This way," Rook said.

Nari followed without asking. She had not asked once. She had not asked when the crew arrived, not when Rook named the timeline, not when Ekow had asked for the closed door. She was a person who had been waiting for this moment for fifteen years and had packed her bag three years ago and had known, at some level below the knowledge she could name, exactly how it would begin.

Her Z6 address registration was under a different name. Jin Mirae. A cover she had held for three years, the civilian infrastructure of a departure she had been planning as long as she had been packed.

The corridor was quiet around them. The building asleep. The bag's strap settled on Nari's shoulder, the weight distributed the way she had learned to carry it over years of practicing departure in the same room.

They moved toward the stairwell.

Behind them, the photograph remained on the shelf — a man and a woman at a table, an ordinary Tuesday, the kind that mattered. The door to the apartment was closed. The room was as it had been, except for the absence of the bag by the door. That absence, in an apartment that had been maintained in a specific state for three years, was the most visible thing in it.

[Beat 16]
The approach pattern arrived in Vox's channel at twenty-three forty-seven.

Rook heard the shift before Vox spoke — the particular quality that four-channel intelligence had when one of the channels developed priority data and the others narrowed to make room for it. The channel contracted. Then Vox.

"Thorn contact signature. Three blocks west of your position, moving east on the arterial. Pattern consistent with a two-person contact deployment in surveillance protocol." A pause of two seconds, which was Vox running the route geometry. "ETA to Nari's building at current pace is sixteen minutes if they stay on the arterial. Twelve if they take the cross-street cutoff."

Rook looked at Ekow.

"They'll take the cross-street," he said. "It's the standard approach for the eastern sector."

"Twelve minutes," she said. "Scout."

"I have it," Scout said. She was in the Z6 maintenance crawlway two levels below the building's occupied floors, the threading interface deployed, two crawlers active in the network she had been mapping since they entered. "I can thread the building's surveillance nodes from here and build a window on the cross-street approach. I need ninety seconds."

"Build it," Rook said.

She was on the building's second floor with Nari, three doors from the maintenance access panel that connected to the crawlway network. The corridor was quiet — Z6 residential at twenty-three forty-seven, the specific quiet of people asleep in rooms they had been in long enough to trust. The extraction had been orderly until the channel shift. The path was mapped, the egress was clear, the buffer boundary was eleven minutes at a careful pace.

Eleven minutes if they moved now. Twelve minutes before the contact team reached them if they took the cross-street.

The margin was not comfortable.

"Boiler," she said.

"On the west stairwell," Boiler said, from one level below. "I can hear exterior door activity on the ground floor if it develops."

"If the contact team reaches the entrance before we're clear."

"I know," he said. The response carried no additional information because it didn't need to. He had already done the calculation.

Ekow was already moving toward the stairwell. Not running — the building was mostly asleep and running produced the kind of sound that woke people and woke people produced the kind of complication that cost minutes they did not have. He moved at the specific pace that covered ground quickly without announcing itself.

"The east service corridor," he said, turning briefly, voice low. "Off the east stairwell, connecting to the maintenance access on the exterior north face. If the contact team enters through the main entrance, the east service corridor gives lateral movement away from their approach entirely. It's four doors from here."

"Scout," Rook said.

"The east service corridor connects to the same crawlway network," Scout said. Her voice had the particular quality of the crawlways — close, the building's structure audible around it. "I'm at the junction. If you move to the east corridor, I can thread you into the maintenance exterior without using the main egress at all."

"The contact team will check the interior once they're in," Vox said. "They'll ping the building's Lotus surveillance nodes for recent mesh activity. If Nari's neuretics have been active in the apartment in the last four hours, the ping confirms position."

Rook looked at Nari. She was standing with the bag on her shoulder, watching the channel conversation happen in the faces of the people around her with the specific attention of someone building context from visible cues rather than audible ones — she had the shape of the situation but not the detail, and she was not filling the gaps with anxiety. She was waiting.

"Your neuretics," Rook said.

"Passive-only since Adaeze contacted me," Nari said. "No active transmission for six days."

"Passive-only is below the Lotus node ping threshold," Ekow said, already at the east stairwell door. "The ping looks for active mesh signatures. She won't register." He held the door. "We need to move."

Rook moved with Nari. Three doors east in the corridor — the maintenance access panel on the left, utility paint, the handle hardware that maintenance contractors used and residents ignored. She pulled it open. Behind it: a short passage, four meters, then a descent ladder into the crawlway network. The space was not built for comfort. It was built for maintenance tech and equipment and nothing else.

She went first, to the base of the ladder, and steadied herself in the crawlway.

Nari did not hesitate. She transferred the bag to her front-carry grip, came down the ladder with the efficiency of someone who was frightened and was not letting the fear direct the movement, and landed at the bottom without sound.

Ekow came in behind her and pulled the panel closed.

"West stairwell," Boiler said, in the channel. "Ground floor entrance has movement. They're at the door."

"We have forty seconds to the junction," Scout said. "Moving."

The crawlway was original 2180s installation — two meters of clearance, the conduit bundles running overhead in iron cable management brackets, the junction lights amber at intervals, the air cool with the specific quality of infrastructure operating in the dark beneath a building that was asleep above it. Forty years of the same function. The brackets' iron steady overhead, the cable runs organized in the patterns someone had laid down four decades ago and nobody had needed to change.

Scout was ahead, one crawler running point and one threading the building's internal surveillance loop. Rook could see the forward crawler's indicator blinking in the amber light — a small threading unit doing its work with the impersonal efficiency of hardware that had no opinion about what it was being used for.

"Contact team is through the main entrance," Vox said. "West stairwell access, ground floor. Running surveillance protocol — they're pinging the building's Lotus nodes first, not proceeding immediately."

This was what Ekow had said they would do. They did it.

"Boiler," Rook said.

What Boiler did in the next ninety seconds was not reported to the team channel. Rook heard it from below and to the west: the sound of something requiring attention, of two people encountering a situation that required them to stop and process it before continuing in the direction they had been going. Not a confrontation — something that produced a pause, a reorientation, the specific quality of a problem that looked like an accident and produced the same effect as an obstacle. It lasted ninety seconds. Then it was over.

"Two minutes," Boiler said. "I'm on the east stairwell. Coming down."

"Scout," Rook said.

"Exterior access twelve meters ahead," Scout said. "I'm at the hatch. The maintenance access opens to the service lane on the north face — it runs outside the Thorn patrol corridor. The nearest patrol team is at the western arterial crossing." A pause. "I've threaded the two corridor nodes on the approach route into a passive loop. They'll register ambient readings, not active traffic."

Twelve meters, ten. Scout's second crawler visible on the crawlway floor ahead — the indicator blinking, map routine running. Five meters. The maintenance hatch at the passage's end.

Scout was at the hatch with the seal hardware already disengaged — the magnetic lock on the interior, the weather seal on the exterior, everything prepared for a one-motion open that would produce no more sound than a door being opened in a building where doors were opened. She looked at Rook. She held up three fingers, two, one.

She opened the hatch.

The north service lane was cold and dark and empty in the way that Z6 produced at twenty-three fifty-three on a weeknight: residents asleep, the night commerce two blocks south, the Thorn patrol at the western arterial crossing where Scout had put them. The air came in with the smell of the buffer zone's ambient — ferrocement and the canal two blocks east and the quiet of a neighborhood that had been sleeping for hours.

Rook stepped out. Nari stepped out behind her. Scout followed with both crawlers, the threading equipment retracting with the efficient motion of hardware that had done what it needed to do. Ekow came through last and brought the hatch to its closed position.

"Boiler," Rook said.

"Coming through the east stairwell," Boiler said. "Thirty seconds."

She waited. The service lane. The cold and the dark and the sound of the city at rest. The bag on Nari's shoulder, settled into the carry position — the weight she had been managing for three years, now outside, in a lane in Z6, with the Thorn patrol at the western arterial and eight minutes of coverage-gap ahead.

The east stairwell exterior door opened. Boiler came through. He looked at Rook, at the lane, at the direction they were going.

"Clean," he said.

"Clean," she confirmed.

She took the lead position and the crew organized itself around the extraction geometry that covered Nari without surrounding her — the specific arrangement of six people moving through a city at night that read as a group, not a convoy, walking, not running, people with a destination rather than people leaving one. Ekow fell in at the rear, the position that covered the approach they had come from. Scout had the crawlers stowed. Boiler at the right shoulder. Lace at the left.

Eight minutes to the buffer boundary.

"The contact team is doing a floor check," Vox said, in the channel. "They're on the second floor. They found the apartment."

"And?" Rook said.

"The apartment is closed. Passive mesh, no active signatures, no flag on the Lotus node. Nari's residency registration shows her as a standard passive-mode user. They're not reading an absence yet — they're reading a resident who's asleep." A pause. "They'll read it as an absence in approximately four minutes when the floor check doesn't produce a response."

"Four minutes is enough," Rook said.

"Yes," Vox said.

They moved north in the service lane, then east at the first junction, the route Ekow had walked twice in the past three hours and had walked before that in the opposite direction for different purposes. The dead zones where the surveillance coverage failed were exactly where the map said they would be — the northeast corner of Nari's block, eight clean minutes before the sweep corrected, the specific gaps that Ekow had been recording for six years and had never used until tonight.

The city was asleep around them. The clinics were running. The markets were closed. The debt ledger offices were dark at this hour, which was the one hour they were dark.

Four minutes later, by Vox's estimate, the Thorn contact team would report that the target was not in the building and request a route search protocol. The route search would go to the staging facility three blocks west. The staging facility would expand the search perimeter to the buffer boundary. The buffer boundary was where the crew would be in four minutes, stepping through it at the point that Ekow had marked on the map with an asterisk.

Dead zone. Coverage gap. Reliable.

The buffer boundary arrived exactly when it was supposed to arrive. Ekow held his position at the last block and looked back once — back at Old Harbor, the clinic lights, the lane they had come from, the building they had exited through its maintenance hatch — and then he stepped through.

The crew was across the boundary in a spread of forty seconds.

No contact. No alert. The Thorn patrol was at the western arterial, doing exactly what it was doing when Scout had noted it seven minutes ago.

Rook did not stop moving, but she let herself register the completion — the full breath of a job that had gone from orderly to pressured and back to controlled without breaking. The crew was good at this. They had been good at this for three years across three jobs, and the quality of what they were good at had not diminished.

Nari was walking in the space Boiler had made for her, the bag still on her shoulder, her pace matching the crew's without being directed to. She was watching the street ahead with the attention of someone who had waited a long time to be walking on this particular street in this particular direction. She had not said anything since the crawlway.

"Two blocks to the vehicle," Vox said.

"We have it," Rook said.

The vehicle was a Vox contact, the same protocol as the Z3 run: sealed courier format, commercial registration, a driver who had been waiting and would not ask. It was parked at the edge of the buffer zone's transit node, unremarkable at this hour among the handful of late-shift vehicles.

Rook put the crew and Nari in the vehicle and got in last. She pulled the door closed. The vehicle entered the buffer zone's arterial and headed west.

In the building they had left, the Thorn contact team was expanding its search to the service lane. They would find the north face maintenance hatch unlocked. They would note it. They would begin building the route picture that would tell them the direction the target had gone.

By the time they had the direction, the crew would be across three zones.

By the time they had the destination, the direction would not be enough.

Rook leaned back in the sealed vehicle and looked at the ceiling of the cargo compartment and thought about nothing in particular for forty-five seconds, which was the amount of time she had before the next thing required her attention.

Forty-five seconds was enough.

[Beat 17]
The maintenance staircase opened behind a door in the east face of the Old Harbor waterfront building — a door that required knowledge of the door before it required knowledge of the lock. Ekow found it by hand in the dark. He had come here in daylight once to set the orientation, but every subsequent use had been at night, and the panel had been designed for night use: flush with the ferrocement wall, the recessed handle assembly invisible until you placed your palm against the specific section of facade and felt the inset — the depression in the cast surface that indicated where the mechanism sat.

The sequence was mechanical. Depress, rotate left, depress. Not electronic. Old enough to predate the Syndicate's current access management architecture, installed when the Old Harbor waterfront was being built and the question of who needed access to maintenance corridors was answered by giving a code to the maintenance workers and a different code to the people running the supply lines, and keeping the two codes distinct because the maintenance workers were not supposed to know what the supply lines were moving. The design assumption had never been updated because it had never needed to be. It worked.

He had used this door eleven times. In his other life. Running the opposite direction.

The staircase was fifteen steps down, narrow, the walls of it ferrocement on both sides, close enough that his shoulders registered the proximity without touching. The smell of the canal preceded the canal itself by thirty meters — enclosed water, old infrastructure, the Old Harbor canal system's particular chemistry, which was not clean but was honest in the way that things are honest when they have been exactly what they are for forty years without variation. Scout's forward crawler ran point, the threading unit producing the only light: amber indicator at the lead position, the floor visible in its narrow working radius, nothing beyond.

Rook counted the steps. Fifteen down. The landing.

The covered walkway ran east along the canal embankment. The ceiling sat at two meters, the right side open to the canal through iron grating — original installation, the bars four centimeters in diameter, the galvanic treatment worn to the underlying metal in the places where four decades of GLMZ atmosphere had worked consistently at the surface. Not failing. Just present, visibly, the way things are present when they have been exactly themselves for long enough that the time shows. The water was visible below through the grating: dark, carrying the harbor's distant ambient light on its surface, the slow current of the enclosed channel giving the light back without amplifying it.

At intervals, iron cable housings projected from the embankment wall into the walkway space. The supply conduits for the Old Harbor community infrastructure ran through this corridor — power, civic data lines, the physical arteries of a neighborhood built around the canal network and dependent on it in ways not visible from the street above. The clinic on the corner drew its auxiliary power from a conduit that ran through this walkway. The market's maintenance systems connected to the grid at a junction sixty meters east. The residential buildings above the market had their secondary water access through a pipe that ran parallel to the cable housings. All of it behind the face of Old Harbor, below the market level, in a corridor that the community had no reason to know existed and that the Syndicate had installed alongside the community's infrastructure so gradually that the two were now the same structure.

Boiler ducked once at a ceiling junction where the conduit housings reduced the clearance. He found the right posture by the second step and held it.

The diesel smell reached them at a hundred and fifty meters — old engine and enclosed water, the smell of operational maintenance. The dock appeared at the corridor's end: covered, the roof four meters above the water line, the structure in good repair because it was used regularly and things used regularly were maintained. Iron mooring cleats. Iron chain guides. The physical hardware of a facility built to move things quietly and that had been moving things quietly for twelve years.

The boat was twelve meters, hull low in the water, the maintenance-vessel configuration that appeared on the harbor grid as exactly what it claimed to be. GLMZ Harbor Services registration, a maintenance coding number, the standard identification. The engine was old enough to be audible and well-maintained enough to be steady — the sound of machinery that had been serviced on schedule, that had been taken care of because it was useful and useful things were taken care of, the operational logic that was exactly the same logic the Syndicate applied to the community infrastructure above them.

The operator was at the mooring line. She was a woman in her fifties with the weathered quality of someone who had been doing this specific work in this specific harbor long enough that the harbor's particular light and atmosphere were written into her. She looked at Ekow when the crew emerged from the corridor.

She looked at only Ekow. She handed him the line and moved to the control position without speaking. The transaction was complete in the exchange of a look and a rope.

Rook organized the crew onto the boat by position — Boiler at the stern, Vox and Lace amidships, Scout at the starboard rail where her crawlers had immediate access to the hull exterior and the harbor perimeter. Rook took the port midship position and watched Nari board last, stepping from the dock to the hull with the controlled movement of someone whose body was engaged in the task while their attention was somewhere else entirely.

Nari looked at the harbor.

The dock's opening faced east. Beyond the covered structure: the canal mouth, the open harbor, the GLMZ at one in the morning. The Spine aeroplex at 480 meters putting its light into the sky above the aeroplex tier, the western shore reflecting the city's operational output into the dark water. The lake — the same lake. From a Z3 diner window, it was a fact you existed alongside, something that had been there before you arrived and would be there when you were gone, the quality of established things. From a maintenance dock at zero-hundred, it was immediate and cold and it was the thing you were crossing to reach whatever came next.

Nari stood at the midship rail and watched it.

Ekow untied the mooring line and took the bow position. The operator ran the vessel off the dock in a single clean departure — no wasted motion, the channel geometry internalized. The hull cleared the mooring hardware. The dock fell back.

The boat moved east.

The silence came with the motion. Not arranged silence — the natural silence of a crew in the aftermath of an operation that had run from orderly to pressured to controlled in the space of twelve minutes and was now done. Nobody had anything to add to what had just happened. Performing relief or satisfaction would have been a lie about what was in the room, and the crew did not deal in those lies. The extraction was complete. The witness was aboard. Whatever needed to be processed would be processed later, when the operation was secured and the accounting could happen in conditions that permitted it.

Scout had her crawlers running the hull exterior — the indicator lights visible at the waterline. Boiler watched the direction they had come from. Vox had something on her monitoring thread, low-priority, not surfaced. Lace was reading the western shoreline with the attention she brought to any place she was preparing to wear. 

Ekow stood at the bow and did not turn around.

He had run this route eleven times in the other direction. Not this exact water, not this exact hour — the supply transfers had run during daylight maintenance windows, the boat registered as scheduled harbor maintenance, the cargo manifested as equipment. He had ridden the boat six of those eleven times, had stood at a position on the hull that was not the bow because the bow was the operator's domain in the daytime configuration. He knew the harbor's thirty-two-minute crossing time because he had counted it, the first time, and had never needed to count it again.

The things he had moved through this corridor were in the list. Not all of them — the route had been used for supplies he knew nothing about, materials that moved before and after his involvement, the continuous operational traffic of an organization that ran its logistics through infrastructure that looked like something else. But some of what had moved here he knew specifically. He had been present for the loading.

None of it was reversible.

The boat ran the inner harbor crossing in twenty-two minutes and the outer crossing in ten more. Thirty-two minutes precisely, the way the harbor's geography produced when you ran it at maintenance-vessel speed on the standard channel route.

Nari did not move from her position at the midship rail for twenty-eight of those minutes. She was watching the water — the surface below the hull, the way the boat's passage disturbed the harbor and the disturbance closed behind them as if the boat had not been there. Not processing it. Watching it the way a person watches something when they are not trying to do anything with what they see. The specific quality of attention that a person uses when they need to be present somewhere without being anywhere in particular.

At twenty-eight minutes, she turned from the water and looked at Ekow's back.

He was still at the bow. Still not turned. She looked at him for a long time, the way she had looked at him in the corridor outside her apartment when Rook had given them the room — with something that was not recognition and was not quite its absence either. The quality of two people who are carrying similar weight in different configurations, and who can identify the weight in each other by its visible effect on the posture, the stillness, the specific way a person occupies a space when they are not occupying it lightly.

She looked at him and he did not turn and after a moment she looked back at the water.

Rook had watched this without examining it.

The Z3 shoreline appeared ahead. The commercial waterway access: covered berth, overnight maintenance slot, the contact Vox had arranged two hours ago. The operator brought the hull in with the same efficiency she had applied to the departure, and held the line while the crew disembarked in the order they had come — Rook first, then Nari, then the others.

Ekow came off last.

He stood on the dock for a moment after the others had moved toward the transit node. His back was to the rest of the crew. He was facing the open harbor past the canal mouth — the water, the lake, the distance. Not the direction they had come from. The direction that contained the horizon.

He put his hand in his coat pocket. The hand stopped when it found what it was looking for. He did not take anything out. The motion was the check-carry — confirming the presence of something without examining it, the habitual verification of weight. Whatever was in the pocket was still there, had been there all night, would be there in the morning.

He removed his hand. He turned from the harbor and walked toward the transit node.

Rook had seen this from thirty meters. She looked away when he turned.

"Vehicle is staged," Vox said, in the channel. "Z3 commercial registration, covered cargo, overnight route."

"We have it," Rook said.

The vehicle was at the edge of the transit node, sealed compartment, the driver's face belonging to someone who had been paid to not know what was in the back. The crew organized itself into the compartment. The door sealed. The vehicle moved north.

Old Harbor was behind them. The canal corridor closed. The dock was already recording the boat's departure as routine maintenance completion — a harbor services vessel completing its overnight run, returning to its registered mooring, nothing to note. The Syndicate's supply infrastructure, run for twelve years to move cargo toward the Lotus's operations, had spent thirty-two minutes running one person away from them. The direction was the only difference. The infrastructure was the same.

Nari sat with the bag in her lap, her hands on the strap, her eyes on the cargo compartment's ceiling. She had been ready for three years for the night she would board a boat at zero-hundred and cross the harbor. She had packed the bag carefully. She had imagined the departure, the crossing, the moment of leaving.

She had not imagined the silence. You could not know to plan for that in advance.

The vehicle moved north through the Z3 arterial. Eleven minutes to the safe house. The city continued around them, the GLMZ at zero-one-thirty doing what it always did — running the infrastructure, keeping the services functional, maintaining the systems that kept the lights on, not asking who they were for.

The silence held for the whole eleven minutes.

Nobody broke it. There was nothing to add.

## The Protocol Named

[Beat 18]
The Z3 safe house was a basement space Rook had used twice before — not a fixed asset, a rotating address maintained by a contact she paid a retainer to on a quarterly schedule. The contact was reliable in the specific way that retainer relationships produced reliability: the address was there when she needed it, in the condition she needed it in, and the contact asked no questions because the retainer price included the value of not asking. The space was not comfortable. It had two chairs that matched and four that didn't, a table that had been level in 2221 and had been compensating for the building's foundation shift ever since, and a single overhead light on the backup circuit because the primary had a fault that the contact had never gotten around to repairing.

Rook had stopped noticing the fault two visits ago. The light it produced was adequate. The adequacy was enough.

Nari slept four hours. Rook did not. She used the four hours for the operational close-out that needed to happen before the next operational phase could begin — the extraction route review, the Thorn contact team's search expansion tracked via Vox's monitoring, the Z3 commercial dock's registration records checked through the harbor grid to confirm the boat's return was logged correctly. It was. The boat had returned to its registered mooring at 0150, the harbor maintenance record clean, nothing to flag.

She did the close-out. She closed the loops. She sat in the backup light and thought about Old Harbor until the thinking resolved into the operational question it needed to resolve into, and then she wrote down the operational question on a piece of paper and put the paper in her coat pocket next to her field kit and waited for Nari to wake up.

Nari came into the main room at 0547. She had slept in her clothes, which were the clothes she had been wearing when the crew arrived at her apartment, which were the clothes she had planned to leave in. Her hair was down and her posture was what it had been since the beginning: upright without being rigid, the specific composure of a person who had been holding themselves in a state of readiness for a long time and had not relaxed the posture even after the moment they had been ready for had finally arrived.

She looked at Rook.

"Now," she said.

"Yes," Rook said.

The crew was at the table. They had organized themselves without direction — Scout against the east wall with her threading equipment in sleep mode, Boiler in the corner with the uneven leg, Vox with the monitoring thread running at low priority and the rest of her attention on the room, Lace to Rook's left, Ekow to Rook's right but closer to Nari's end of the table. He had placed himself there without discussion.

Nari sat at the far end and put her hands in her lap.

"I'll begin with the structure," she said. "Because the structure is what makes the rest of things I need to say make sense."

She began with the Stems. The Lotus Syndicate's governing council — not the name the Syndicate used publicly, which was Lotus Community Leadership Council, but the operational name used internally, which was what she was going to use because she was done using their public names. Blood-purity gated membership: heritage verification, two-generation bloodline record with no exception procedure. She had been an adjutant to a Stem named Jang Yong-su for seven years. The adjutant role was administrative: she attended the meetings, she took the notes, she managed the record. She was present in the room for every vote that was recorded.

"I was present for the Protocol vote," she said. "In 2211. I was twenty-eight years old."

The crew was still. Not politely still — the specific stillness of people who had decided before the conversation started that their job was to receive the information, that the information's weight required reception rather than commentary, that the discipline of not interrupting was itself a form of respect for how long she had been carrying this.

She continued.

"They called it the Purification Protocol from the founding document," she said. "The name was not changed between internal and external records. The word 'purification' appears in the founding document's header. The document was drafted in 2186. I have not seen the original draft — I was not born in 2186. I saw the active version in 2211, which was the fourth revision. The fourth revision contained language describing the revisions' history. I read it. I memorized what I could."

"Why," Rook said.

"Because I understood, when I left, that my memory of it was the only copy I would be able to carry."

The room received this. Eight seconds of nothing, which was the silence of six people registering what it meant that a woman had spent fifteen years memorizing a document's key provisions because she had understood that the document was evidence and that the evidence existed only where she could keep it.

Nari did not acknowledge the silence. She continued.

The Protocol's development had four phases, all of them preparatory. Phase 0 was operational now — she learned this from Adaeze's information two months ago, not from direct knowledge; she had been out of the Syndicate's information flow since 2211. Phase 0 was the test deployment: a six-month period in which the Z6 civic grid cluster had been accessed through a cooperative arrangement with a Civic Systems Engineer, the neuretics registry queried for 200,000 Z6 registered individuals, the data extracted and stored in a Lotus-controlled archive. The Phase 0 deployment had been described to the GLMZ Civic Bureau as a routine system test. The Bureau had accepted this description because the Bureau processed twelve thousand routine system test notifications annually and had the staffing to review approximately four hundred of them.

"The partial rollback succeeded for the secondary nodes," she said. "Three nodes did not roll back. The 200,000 records are in Lotus possession now." She said this with the same level voice she had used for everything else, the voice of a person who had organized the facts she was carrying into a presentable sequence and was presenting them in that sequence, the emotion that accompanied the facts carried alongside them but not mixed into the delivery. "Phase 1 requires three components. I can describe them because I was in the room when they were discussed."

She paused. One breath.

"Demographic baseline — a comprehensive heritage cross-section of the Gray Zone's resident population, covering at minimum fifteen years of migration data. The founding document describes this as a population registry: a data set that allows Phase 1 operations to begin not by census, which would require authorization and leave a public record, but by cross-reference. If you have fifteen years of migration patterns, debt distributions, and heritage cross-sections in a civic-grid schema, you can identify every household in the Z4 through Z7 corridor that meets the displacement criteria without conducting a single official inquiry." She did not pause between items. "Neuretics registry access — the means of querying the GLMZ civic registry for individual heritage classification independent of the individual's consent or knowledge. The demographic baseline gives you neighborhoods and household distributions. The registry access gives you individual identities. Phase 1 operations can then target specific people, not areas." Another breath. "A deployment window — the founding document identified the GLMZ neuretics system's generation update as the relevant window. Article 14 of the Meridian Charter requires a thirty-day public review period for any large-scale registry modification. During the generation update, the Charter's review mechanism is suspended for the update's duration. The generation update is scheduled to begin in thirty days."

She looked at the crew.

She looked at all of them in turn, the specific survey of a person who had been carrying a thing for a long time and needed to see the faces of the people she was putting it in front of.

"I was twenty-eight years old when I heard this," she said. "I had been in this role for seven years. I had known for two of those years that the Syndicate's structure included — that the community services and the blood-purity doctrine were not separate departments of the same organization. That they were the same operation. That the clinics and the debt collection and the Phase 1 displacement plan were one plan." She looked at her hands. "I did not know what to do with that knowledge in a way that wasn't either surrender or martyrdom. I left. I filed the Protocol under when I die I will have done nothing and I waited."

"For fifteen years," Boiler said.

"For fifteen years," she said.

The room was quiet. Not a polished quiet, not the kind that required maintenance — the quiet that arrived when the weight of a thing had been set down and was now occupying the space it needed to occupy before anyone moved.

Boiler said, "Thank you."

He said it in his ordinary speaking voice, without ceremony, the way a person says something when they mean it and do not need to add anything to the meaning to convey the sincerity. Two words. He did not elaborate.

Nari looked at him. Something in her face changed — not opened exactly, but arrived at a different distance from closed. She had been carrying this for fifteen years and had imagined the moment she would put it in front of someone and they would understand what the carrying had cost, and in the imagined version the moment had been larger and more structured around the weight of what she had survived. The version in the basement room was a Finnish-Somali structural engineer saying thank you in his ordinary voice, and it was, in some way she had not predicted, exactly the correct version. Not the dramatic one. The true one.

"Yes," she said.

She looked at Rook.

"Ask me what you need to ask."

The questions were operational and Rook asked them in the register Nari handled best. The founding document: she reproduced the key provisions from memory, the specific language of the heritage classification criteria, the Phase 1 operational parameters, the displacement threshold definitions. She had not written them down in fifteen years. She had rehearsed them — not aloud, in the specific internal rehearsal of a person who needed to be sure the text was still there, the way you check a thing you are responsible for maintaining. The text was there. It had always been there. She recited it without error.

The vote record: seven to two, 2211. She named the voters. Jang Yong-su, for. Six others, for. Two against. She named the two against as well. Both had been moved from their Stem positions within eighteen months of the vote, reassigned to administrative roles with no council function. She did not comment on the timing.

The Phase 0 personnel: the administrator who had authorized the civic grid access. She named them by title and Lotus designation. She described the authorization chain: Root-level approval, distributed to the relevant Stem, executed through a Civic Systems Engineer contact. She did not know the engineer's name. She knew the Stem's name. She gave it.

Vox was writing everything down — on paper, the physical transcript, because the operational board was going to be analog until Rook decided it could be otherwise, and that decision had not been made yet. Her handwriting was compact and precise, the pages accumulating.

At 0712, Nari said: "The administrative authorization has Seul-ki's direct signature."

She said the Root's name without inflection, without emphasis, without adding anything to it that would make it more than the name. It was the name of the person who had authorized the Phase 0 deployment and who had authorized the founding document and who had been running the Lotus Syndicate for thirty years — the name of the person at the center of what they were in, which Nari knew and which was why she named her last rather than first, saving the center for last because it was the weight that everything else organized around.

Rook wrote it down.

At 0728, Nari said, "That's everything I have."

She looked at the window — the east-facing basement window, the Z3 street visible through the security glass in the early-morning light. The day beginning. The specific quality of morning light that said: the thing you have been waiting to do is done. You have arrived at the other side of the waiting.

She had waited fifteen years for this specific morning. Not for the extraction, not for the boat, not for the basement room with the uneven table and the mismatched chairs. For the morning that came after it — the morning where the document existed outside her memory, where the fourteen pages of transcript were in someone else's hands, where the weight was distributed rather than carried alone.

The transcript was fourteen pages.

Rook looked at it on the table in front of Vox. Fourteen pages of the Purification Protocol's history, scope, personnel, and operational timeline. Produced by a woman who had memorized a founding document at twenty-eight years old and had kept it intact in her memory for fifteen years while living in a Z6 apartment with a packed bag by the door.

The three components.

They had the demographic baseline. Vox had confirmed it when she read the Headcount data — the data layer was exactly what the Protocol's founding document described as the demographic baseline requirement. The sculpture was the first component, already in their possession, already taken from the client who had commissioned it.

The neuretics registry access was the second. Someone was working on that access right now, somewhere in the GLMZ civic infrastructure, somewhere they had not yet located. The person who had been coerced to provide it, or who was providing it willingly, or who had already provided the test cluster data and was being pressured to provide the full grid access now.

The deployment window was thirty days.

Rook picked up the paper she had written in the night — the operational question. She had written: What do you have when you take apart the load-bearing element without replacing it?

She looked at it for a moment. She put it back in her coat pocket.

"We'll let you rest," she said to Nari.

"I'm not tired," Nari said.

"Rest anyway," Rook said. "We have work to do and we'll need you when we're further along in it. What you just gave us takes time to use correctly."

Nari looked at her. The assessment — brief, pragmatic. She had waited fifteen years. She was not going to argue about rest.

"Yes," she said. She stood. She went back into the sleeping area without further comment.

The crew sat for a moment after she left.

The transcript was fourteen pages. The weight of it was in the room now. Outside Nari's memory, it was a different kind of thing — it was a document, it was evidence, it was the specific gravity of something that had been carried alone for a very long time and had just been set down in the middle of a table that had been level in 2221 and had been imprecisely level ever since.

Nobody rushed to speak. They sat with it for a moment, the way you sit with something that deserves to be sat with before the work of it begins.

Then Vox said, "I need about eleven hours with the Headcount data." She was already reaching for her monitoring equipment, the precision of someone who has identified what comes next and is organized around beginning it. "There's something I want to cross-reference."

"Do it," Rook said.

Vox began.

The overhead light ran on the backup circuit. The building above them was starting its morning. In the sleeping area, through the door that had been left slightly open, Nari's breathing settled into the rhythm of a person who was, after all, tired — who had carried something for fifteen years and had set it down and had, in the setting down, found out what rest was.

[Beat 19]
Vox worked through the morning and into the afternoon. She did not summarize her progress in the channel and nobody asked her to. The crew operated in the practiced patience of people who understood that analysis produced what it produced when it was done rather than when you needed it to be done, and that asking Vox to report before she was ready to report was the equivalent of asking Boiler to give you the load calculation before he had run it. The calculation ran when it ran. The result was worth waiting for.

At 1634, she looked up from the equipment.

"I need the table," she said.

Rook cleared the table. The crew moved their chairs. Vox spread four sheets of paper across the surface — not electronic output, not a display projection, physical paper with handwritten analysis and printed data extracts she had pulled from the gray-market archives and annotated. The four channels visible in the notation: each sheet was assigned a channel-color in the margin, the four-color coding she had been using since the rebuild, the narrower network's discipline applied to how she organized the information she retrieved through it.

She had been at four channels since the graze wound. Not because the rebuild had stopped at four — she could have continued. She had chosen to stop at four because four was the number where the information came in ordered rather than in volume, where she processed what she received rather than tracking what she had missed, where the network was an instrument rather than an environment. She had made the decision explicitly, after the rebuild, and had not revisited it. Four was the correct number. This analysis had been run through four channels and had produced, in eleven hours, a connection that she was confident was structural rather than coincidental.

She indicated the first sheet.

"The Headcount sculpture's data layer," she said. "Fifteen years of Gray Zone demographics in civic-grid schema. The specific schema is GLMZ Census Infrastructure Version 4.2 — which is the census schema from 2211, the year the GLMZ retired the official heritage classification field from the public census record. The field was removed from the public record in compliance with the Meridian Charter's data equity provisions." She paused. "It was not removed from the underlying civic-grid database. It was reclassified as restricted infrastructure data. The infrastructure version of the schema still contains it."

She let this sit for a moment.

"The Headcount data is collected and structured in the infrastructure version of the schema," she said. "Not the public version. The data contains the heritage classification field. It contains fifteen years of heritage classification data for the Z4 through Z7 corridor — migration patterns, household compositions, neuretics registration status, debt distribution by heritage classification, everything that the public census stopped counting publicly in 2211."

"Because in 2211 the public census retired the heritage field," Vox said, to herself as much as to the room. It was the thing she had not said yet, the thing that clicked the timing into place. "And the Headcount data covers 2211 forward. The sculpture starts where the public record stops."

"Fifteen years," Ekow said. He said it with the quality of a man completing a calculation he had already run and arriving at the same answer again.

"Exactly fifteen years," Vox said. "The sculpture's oldest data entry is dated two months after the Meridian Charter's heritage field retirement. The collection did not begin earlier. It began exactly when the public record ended." She moved to the second sheet. "Which is what the Protocol's founding document describes as the demographic baseline requirement. A data set that picks up where the official record stops. A population registry that can exist without official authorization because it is structured around a field the official record no longer acknowledges."

Scout said, "The sculpture was built for this."

"Yes," Vox said. "The sculpture was not commissioned to document Gray Zone demographics. It was commissioned to construct the Protocol's first Phase 1 requirement. The art commission was the data collection vehicle." She looked at the table, at the four sheets. "The commission was placed with the Gray Horizon Collective in 2213. The Gray Horizon Collective is a Z4 art installation and civic documentation group — the commission would not have appeared unusual to them. The brief specified data format: civic-grid schema. Structured data integrated into physical sculpture. Public installation in a Z3 collector's market."

"Publicly visible," Rook said.

"A public installation creates legal ambiguity about ownership," Vox said. "If the data is public — if anyone can read the sculpture's data layer — it is not a private registry. It is a public artwork with accessible data. The Meridian Charter's data privacy provisions do not apply to public artworks in the same way they apply to private databases." She paused. "The sculpture is both simultaneously. Publicly available, privately useful. The data is there for anyone who can read it. The only people who have the means to read it the way it needs to be read are the people who commissioned it."

Lace looked at the table. "An entire data collection methodology disguised as a public art installation," she said, and her voice had a quality Rook did not hear from her often — not quite contempt, something more specific than contempt. The quality of a person who had spent their career being the only one in the room who understood the gap between presentation and reality, encountering an institution that had been exploiting that gap at scale, systematically, for decades.

"The same methodology," Lace said, "at a different scale."

"Yes," Vox said.

She moved to the third sheet.

"The second component: neuretics registry access." She had an extract from the Z6 civic grid test cluster's administrative logs — gray-market, from an archive she was not going to name because the archive's provenance was its own thing entirely and naming it would add a complication the room did not need right now. "The Phase 0 deployment. Six months. The 200,000 Z6 registered neuretics records in Lotus possession. This is confirmed by what Nari described and by the administrative log signature." She indicated a line of notation. "The deployment was run through a Civic Systems Engineer. Grade 4. The access authorization required a Civic Systems Engineer's credentials — the neuretics infrastructure is Grade 4 minimum for direct registry queries, per the Meridian Charter's technical provisions."

"They coerced someone," Rook said.

"That's the operational implication," Vox said. "The test deployment required ongoing cooperation from a Grade 4 engineer. Six months. The scale and duration of the deployment is not consistent with a single-event breach — it required repeated access over the six-month window. Someone inside the infrastructure was providing it." She indicated the fourth sheet. "The deployment window. The generation update. Thirty days from the current date. The neuretics system's generation update begins in thirty days. During the update, Article 14 review is suspended. If the full neuretics registry backdoor — not the test cluster, the full grid — is in Lotus possession before the update begins, they can run the complete Phase 1 query set during the update window, and the results will be delivered before the review mechanism restores."

She stopped.

She looked at the four sheets on the table and then at the crew.

"The three components," she said. "Demographic baseline: the Headcount sculpture. Currently in our possession. Second: neuretics registry access. The Syndicate is acquiring it. The coerced engineer is providing the access pathway, presumably under continued pressure, with the full deployment timed for the generation update window. Third: the window itself. Thirty days."

The room was quiet for a moment that was different from the quiet after Nari's briefing — denser, more operational. The Nari briefing had been the weight of knowing what the Protocol was. This was the weight of understanding what the Protocol needed to work and which of those things the Syndicate already had.

"We have the first component," Rook said.

"We have it," Vox said. "It is sitting in the Z3 safe house in a sealed cargo case. The Lotus commissioned it, paid for it, and arranged its recovery. The fee they paid to Ferris, which Ferris paid to you, came from the Lotus's operational budget." She looked at Rook. "The Φ12,000 commission paid by the anonymous client was funding from the Purification Protocol's preparatory budget. The job we took to recover the sculpture was a job the Protocol authorized to recover its own first component."

Rook had known this since the Z3 collector's market. Not the full detail — not the data schema, not the Phase 0 connection, not the precise mechanism. But the shape of it had been visible since Vox opened the data layer and said this is not art. She had known it was connected. She had not known how precisely.

The precision was the thing.

"The sculpture is structured to Phase 1 requirements," she said. "Exactly."

"Exactly," Vox said. "The data format, the heritage field inclusion, the fifteen-year coverage starting from the Charter's retirement of the public heritage record, the civic-grid schema version — all of it is specified in the Protocol's founding document as the demographic baseline requirement. The sculpture is not analogous to the requirement. It is the requirement, produced on commission, installed in a Z3 market under cover of public art, and available for Lotus acquisition when they needed it." She began gathering the sheets, the four-channel notation, the gray-market archive extracts. "The sculpture's collector had no idea what he was holding. He thought he was sitting on a high-value art investment with a complex provenance question. He was sitting on the Protocol's first operational asset."

Ekow said, "Who organized the collection?"

"The commission brief came from a Z5 cultural development foundation," Vox said. "The foundation was established in 2212 — one year before the commission was placed. The foundation's incorporation documents list three directors. Two of them have no other institutional affiliations. The third is a former Stem assistant."

Nobody asked her to confirm the connection. The connection was confirmed.

"How long did we have the sculpture before the Thorn team ran the surveillance flag," Scout said.

"Park was watching it for three weeks before we arrived," Vox said. "He noted our entry and exit. He did not move."

"Because moving the surveillance would have cost them the sculpture," Rook said. "They needed us to take it first. They were waiting for the recovery operation so they could recover it from us."

She thought about the timeline of the Thorns reaching Nari's building. 2347. The same night as the market run, which had been — she checked the operational timeline in her head — three days prior. Three days between the sculpture recovery and the Thorn approach on Nari. The timing was not coincidental.

"Park flagged us at the market," she said. "The Thorn deployment on Nari was three days later. He ran a cross-reference."

"Adaeze's referral came through his network," Ekow said. "Not directly. But the referral chain exists — anyone looking at the right connections could map the route from Adaeze's legal cooperative to the crew she might call." He said this without affect, the acknowledgment of how Lotus intelligence worked when it was working correctly.

"He moved on Nari when he identified the crew," Rook said. "Not because the sculpture triggered it — because the crew that took the sculpture was also the crew with connections to Adaeze, who had connections to Nari. He wasn't chasing the sculpture. He was chasing whoever came for it."

The four sheets were stacked on the table. Vox had her equipment in standby.

The safe house was quiet around them. The uneven table. The mismatched chairs. The backup light running its steady inadequate output, sufficient and nothing more.

Three components. One in their possession. One in active development by the organization they had been unknowingly working for. One a countdown that was running whether they acted on it or not.

"Thirty days," Boiler said. He said it with the specific quality he brought to structural pronouncements — not fatalism, not alarm. The voice of a man who had run the numbers and was stating the result. "If the generation update begins in thirty days and the full registry access is delivered before it begins, the Phase 1 query runs during the update window before review restores."

"Yes," Vox said.

"And the Phase 0 data — the 200,000 Z6 records — is available now," he said. "Combined with the Headcount baseline."

"Enough to begin targeted displacement in Z6 immediately," Vox said. "Before Phase 1. The Phase 0 data set is smaller but it covers Z6 comprehensively. They do not need Phase 1 to begin Z6 operations. Phase 1 expands the scope to the full Z4 through Z7 corridor."

Boiler looked at the table. He looked at nothing in particular on the table, the gaze of a man running a structural analysis in his head that was not a structural analysis. He did not say what he was thinking. He did not need to.

"We need to think about what comes next," Rook said.

Nobody disagreed.

The afternoon light was visible at the top of the basement window — the Z3 street in its late-afternoon function, the city doing what it always did outside of whatever was happening in basements and harbor corridors and maintenance crawlways. The city did not know what they were sitting in. It was running the infrastructure, maintaining the services, keeping the lights on, and not asking what was underneath.

Vox put her four sheets in the operational file.

The Headcount sculpture was in the sealed cargo case in the corner of the room. The first component of a plan forty years in the making, currently inside a Z3 safe house, in the custody of the crew that had been paid by the plan's authors to retrieve it.

The lines were drawn. Vox had put them there. The crew had watched them connect.

[Beat 20]
Rook stood at the table with the four sheets in front of her and the crew seated and looked at the window for a moment. Not through it — the basement window showed the Z3 street at ankle height, the late-afternoon pedestrian traffic moving past in fragments, the city's working rhythm visible in the passing shoes and wheels. She looked at the window without seeing the street. She was looking at the geometry of the thing.

Then she turned and looked at the crew.

"We're in a genocide intervention," she said.

She said it plainly, without the verbal scaffolding that made a statement easier to deliver. Plain was the correct register for it. The statement did not require softening and she was not going to soften it, because softening it would have been a lie about what it was, and she had learned — at some cost, across three jobs with this crew and whatever she had been before those jobs — that lies about what things were tended to produce operational failures at the worst possible moment.

Nobody in the room spoke.

Not because the statement was a surprise — she could see from the quality of the crew's stillness that each of them had been sitting with some version of this conclusion since the Nari briefing, some since earlier than that. Boiler had been building the calculation since the walk through Old Harbor. Ekow had been building it for twelve years. Vox had been building it since she opened the Headcount data. They knew what they were in. They had been sitting with knowing it while they waited for the room that named it.

She named it.

"The Lotus Syndicate has been planning a systematic displacement operation for forty years," she said. "The demographics for it are in that cargo case. The administrative access to execute it is being coerced from someone inside the GLMZ civic infrastructure who we have not yet located. The deployment window opens in thirty days. The Φ12,000 we accepted from the anonymous client was from the Protocol's operational budget." She paused. "We have been employed by the entity funding its own dismantlement. Whether or not that fact is going to remain true depends on what we do in the next thirty days."

Scout said, "The Ferris job was bait."

"Not quite," Rook said. "The Ferris job was a recovery operation the Syndicate needed completed. The sculpture was out of their possession and they needed it back. They commissioned the recovery because they needed it done. The fact that the crew doing the recovery was also connected to Adaeze's network, which connected to Nari, was a problem that Park identified after the fact. It wasn't designed. It was a coincidence that became a vulnerability."

"They were going to retrieve the sculpture from us," Ekow said.

"Yes. After we did the work of taking it from the collector. That was the plan." She looked at the sealed cargo case in the corner. "The collector had been sitting on it for three months, not selling it, waiting for the price to go up. From the Syndicate's perspective, the recovery crew was a tool for solving the problem of a collector who was holding out. Once we had the sculpture, they would have applied pressure to recover it." She considered this. "Park's surveillance flag was the first step in that process. The Thorn deployment on Nari was the second — he was moving to neutralize the connection between the recovery crew and Adaeze before the recovery crew became a problem. He was three days too slow."

"Because we moved on Nari the same day Adaeze called," Lace said.

"Yes."

The room sat with this for a moment. The operational picture, fully assembled.
