The Ferris job as the entry point, the Nari extraction as the intervention, the Lotus not knowing that the crew it had contracted to recover its demographic baseline was now in possession of that baseline and the testimony of the person who had witnessed its authorization vote.

"The anonymous client," Vox said. "They still believe we're holding the sculpture pending the exchange."

"They believed it until the Thorn deployment on Nari," Rook said. "At that point, Park's operational assessment would have flagged us as a complication rather than a tool. The client — whoever placed the Ferris contract on the Syndicate's behalf — now knows the recovery crew is not going to deliver the sculpture."

"They know where the Z3 safe house is," Scout said. It was not a question. It was a structural assessment of the situation's exposure, the kind of observation Scout made when she was building the operational picture in her head and the next step was to identify what they were exposed to.

"They have the Z3 address from the Ferris routing chain if they traced it," Rook said. "We move tomorrow morning. Vox has the secondary location."

"Ready," Vox said.

Rook looked at the table.

"The operational question," she said, "is not whether to intervene. We're already in it — we have the sculpture, we have the witness, and the Thorn deployment ran on Nari because of our connection to Adaeze. We have done the thing that puts us in the middle of this regardless of whether we intend to be. The question is operational: what does the intervention look like, and what does it cost, and what survives it."

She let the last part of that sit.

"The Syndicate runs eleven clinics in Z5 and Z6," she said. "The free clinics. The community markets — seven of them in the Z4 through Z7 corridor. The debt management structure that is simultaneously the mechanism of leverage and the mechanism of housing preservation for approximately forty thousand people in the buffer zone who could not maintain their addresses without the Lotus's debt restructuring services. The community building where Boiler has been working is dependent on the Lotus's load-bearing infrastructure investment." She looked at Boiler. "Tell them."

He did not hesitate. "The community center building in Z5/Z6 has a load-path dependency on the market infrastructure that was integrated in 2209," he said. "The market columns are structural supports for the residential above. The residential above is the community center's upper-floor operations — the childcare cooperative, the adult education rooms, the medical intake space that one of the free clinics uses as a satellite. If the market infrastructure fails — if the funding that maintains the market columns stops, if the maintenance access that keeps the structural joints operational is removed — the residential above becomes compromised within two to three years." He said this with the same precision he brought to any structural problem. "It is not a dramatic collapse. It is a slow failure. The building becomes unusable before it becomes dangerous. But it becomes unusable."

The room received this.

"The Syndicate is load-bearing," Rook said. "For the physical infrastructure. For the health services. For the debt structures that allow people to stay in their homes. For a community that built around the services and that did not build that way because of any conspiracy — that built that way because the services were there and the services were real and the community responded to real services the way communities respond to real services. The load-bearing was not an accident. It was the design. But the design does not change the weight." She looked at the table. "You cannot remove the load-bearing element without the structure it supports failing."

"Unless you provide an alternative load path," Boiler said.

He said it simply, the way he said structural solutions — as a statement of engineering fact rather than a rhetorical contribution. There was an alternative, in structural terms. You identified the load path. You designed the alternative that could carry the load. You transferred the load to the alternative before you removed the original. You did not remove the load-bearing element and then design the alternative. You designed the alternative first.

"Yes," Rook said. "Unless you provide an alternative load path first."

The room understood this.

"Three threads," she said. "The Headcount sculpture — we have it. The witness — we have her. The third thread is the access pathway. The coerced engineer who is building the neuretics registry backdoor. We don't know who they are yet. We have not located them. But they exist, and they are in the civic infrastructure somewhere, and they are running out of time in the same way we are running out of time, and there is a nonzero probability that they want out of the arrangement they are in." She looked at Vox. "Same probability that the civic infrastructure has a signal leak that we can find if we look for it."

"I have been looking," Vox said. "Nothing yet. I will keep looking."

"Three threads, one source, one plan," Rook said. "The source is the Lotus Syndicate. The plan is the Protocol. The question is not how to destroy the Syndicate — destroying the Syndicate produces the failure of the load-bearing element without a replacement, which produces community collapse in the buffer zone, which is harm done to the same population the Protocol is designed to harm. The question is how to make the Protocol impossible to execute without destroying the thing the Protocol is embedded in."

"Separate the silk from the iron," Lace said.

She said it quietly, not as a point she was making so much as a thing she was thinking aloud, the private notation of a person who was working through a problem in a language they knew well. Rook heard it. She did not respond to it directly.

"Nobody has to make that decision right now," Rook said. "What we have to do right now is find the third thread. The access pathway. The person inside the civic infrastructure who is building the backdoor. We find them before the Syndicate can close the deployment. If we find them, we have all three components of the Protocol under our control before the generation update window opens, and the conversation with the Syndicate happens from that position rather than from nothing."

"The conversation," Ekow said. He said it without editorial inflection, which was itself an editorial note.

"There will be a conversation," Rook said. "There are two possible ends to this. One: we destroy the Protocol completely and accept what that costs in terms of the Syndicate's structure and the community infrastructure that depends on the Syndicate's structure. Two: we give the Syndicate a choice — dismantle the Protocol publicly and fund the transition infrastructure that the community needs to operate without the Protocol's load-bearing contribution, or the documentation goes to public disclosure and the Syndicate loses the community trust that is the only thing making the load-bearing possible in the first place." She paused. "Option two requires the Syndicate to have something to lose. Which means not destroying it prematurely."

"Option two requires someone at the top of the Syndicate to be willing to make that choice," Ekow said.

"Yes," Rook said.

He looked at the table. He was thinking about something he did not say, the specific quality of a person who had knowledge relevant to the conversation they were in and who was deciding when to put it in the room. He did not put it in the room yet.

Rook did not push.

"No one has to commit to either option now," she said. "We don't have all three threads. We don't have enough information. What we do now is find the third thread. Everything else follows from that." She looked at the crew. "The Φ12,000 is not what we're working for. Everybody knows that. The Φ12,000 was a door. We walked through it. We're in the room now. The question of what we do in the room is going to take a minute to answer correctly."

Nobody argued with this.

Lace said, "How long do we have at this address?"

"We move at 0700," Rook said.

"And Nari?"

"She comes with us. She stays with us until we have a resolution. Her testimony is evidence only as long as she is alive to give it." She said this plainly, with the same register she had used for everything else. "She knows this. We discussed it."

Scout said, "The operational board goes analog when we move."

"Already planned," Rook said.

Boiler had been looking at the cargo case in the corner for the past several minutes — not at the case exactly, at the space the case occupied, the way he looked at a structural problem when he was building the load calculation in his head. He looked at it for another moment, then looked at his hands.

"The community building in Z5/Z6," he said. "The renovation I've been running. The childcare cooperative. The medical intake satellite." He looked at Rook. "If this goes wrong — if the Syndicate fractures and the infrastructure fails and there's no transition funding — those people don't have somewhere else to go."

"I know," Rook said.

"So the alternative load path has to exist before we remove the original," he said.

"I know," she said.

He looked at the table. He was not arguing with her — this was not a challenge to the plan, because there wasn't a plan yet, and he understood that. He was adding the weight to the accounting. Making sure it was in the room.

It was in the room. It had been in the room since the walk through Old Harbor, since the market column integration, since the conversation between him and Ekow on the street outside the debt collection office that shared a door with the clinic. He did not need to add it again.

"Right," he said.

The backup light was steady. The Z3 street above the window was moving through its evening function, the city's routine unchanged and unhurried. By Nari's account — intel she had not verified since she had last had access to the Protocol documentation — the generation update would begin in approximately thirty days, and the window that the Protocol had planned around for fifteen years would open.

Twenty-nine days.

Rook had not answered all of it. She had not answered any of it cleanly. What she had done was put the shape of the problem in the room, in the same room as the people who were going to have to solve it, and let the shape be there. The shape did not become smaller for being named. It became clearer.

Clarity was not comfort. Clarity was not a plan. Clarity was what you needed before you could have either.

She picked up the paper from her coat pocket. The operational question she had written at zero-three-hundred. She looked at it. She put it back.

"Get some rest," she said. "We move at 0700."

The crew did not ask about rest. They did what she said, which was the thing they had always done when the operational phase said wait. They waited. They slept when they could sleep. They ran their maintenance checks, their equipment reviews, their individual preparations. They did not talk about what came next because what came next was not yet visible.

In the cargo case in the corner, the Headcount sculpture waited. In the sleeping area, Nari slept. Outside, the GLMZ ran its systems.

Twenty-nine days.

## Priya's Signal

[Beat 21]
The secondary safe house was a Z3 building that had been a textile cooperative until 2219 and had been a mixed-use building with a short-term commercial lease on the ground floor since then. Rook had used it once, for a different job, three years ago. She had noted the back egress, the maintenance access on the north face, the fire-suppression infrastructure that shared a crawlway junction with the building next door. She had not expected to use it again. She had kept the contact's information in the rotation anyway, because useful things were kept.

They had been here four days.

The operational board was on the east wall: paper, Rook's handwriting, physical annotations in the four-color scheme Vox used for her channel analysis. Three columns. The board had been up since the second day and had grown with each session of cross-reference and cross-check, the writing dense in some areas and sparse in others, the specific topography of an analysis that had followed the information rather than a predetermined structure. Rook had looked at it enough times that she could read it from the corner of her eye. The shape of the problem. Present, physical, not going anywhere.

Nari was in the secondary sleeping room, the one that had been the cooperative's fabric storage room and that still smelled of the particular synthetic weave the cooperative had processed — not unpleasant, just specific. She had been sleeping more in the past four days than she had apparently slept in the previous three years. Rook had checked on her twice, found her asleep both times, and had stopped checking. The body knew what it owed itself. The body was paying.

The crew was in the configuration they held at the safe house during active analysis: Vox at the equipment table, channels running, the four-thread monitoring discipline she had maintained since the rebuild. Scout with Gerald on the secondary perimeter sweep — she had been running paired operations since the Old Harbor extraction, the crawler on the outside while her attention was on the inside, the dual-presence that had become habitual rather than deliberate. Ekow was running the Lotus operational pattern review that Rook had asked him to produce — the Z5/Z6 deployment timeline, the Thorn circuit rotation, the administrative contact chain from Root to Stems. He was producing it from memory, which was the only way he had to produce it. Boiler was at the Z5/Z6 community center three blocks from the cooperative on a legitimate renovation schedule that had not stopped for the investigation, because stopping it would have changed the operational signature and because the renovation mattered independently of the investigation.

Rook was at the operational board.

She had been looking at the third column for an hour. The third column had one entry: *neuretics registry access — coerced Grade 4 CSE — unlocated.* The rest of the column was empty in the way that empty spaces in an operational analysis were empty — not blank, waiting. The space of a thing that was out there somewhere and had not been found yet.

At 0317, Vox looked up from the equipment table.

"I have something," she said.

Rook turned from the board.

Vox was looking at her monitoring interface with the quality of attention she brought to things that required confirming before she named them — not excitement, not alarm, the focused neutrality of a person running a second check before the first check produced a conclusion. She ran the second check. She ran a third.

"Civic Systems Engineer guild protocol," she said. "Encrypted distress signal. Z8 civic systems node."

Rook came to the table.

"Not standard gray-market," Vox said. She was pulling the signal's structure onto her paper analysis sheet — the notation she used when something came through the network that needed to be looked at rather than just heard. "The guild protocol is a GLMZ Civic Systems Engineers' mutual aid frequency — an internal professional network maintained since 2208 for engineers needing to flag operational problems that the official reporting channels can't address." She paused. "The official reporting channels can't address them because the problems are with the official systems. The guild protocol exists for the specific scenario where a civic engineer has identified a system compromise that the responsible oversight body is not able to investigate without political exposure."

"A whisper channel," Rook said.

"A professional one," Vox said. "The guild protocol requires a current guild membership certificate to access. It's not a general gray-market channel. It's not accessible without professional credentials. The person sending this signal has real credentials — an active Civic Systems Engineer guild member, Grade 4 minimum, because Grade 3 and below don't have access to the Z8 civic node infrastructure."

"Grade 4 is what the neuretics registry access requires," Rook said.

"Grade 4 minimum," Vox said.

They looked at each other across the equipment table.

"The signal is encrypted," Vox said. "The format is a standard distress structure on the guild frequency — not a content message, a contact request. It says: I exist, I have something, I need someone who can receive this through a channel that is not monitored by the entity I have the problem with. The content of the problem is not in the signal. The signal is just the hand raised in a room that is not supposed to have anyone in it."

"Who does the guild channel route to," Rook said.

"The guild's mutual aid network runs to seven endpoints," Vox said. "Three of the endpoints are the guild's own administrative body — they would be the first contact for standard situations. Two are GLMZ civic oversight contacts, which the guild maintains for escalated situations where the administrative body needs external validation. Two are unaffiliated technical contacts — people or entities who are not part of the guild or the oversight structure but who the guild has determined can be trusted with problem reports that the official channels cannot handle." She paused. "One of those unaffiliated contacts is a gray-market technical broker that has been operating in Z4 for eleven years. The broker maintains a relay that touches my secondary monitoring thread on the Z4 band."

"She sent the signal to the gray-market relay," Rook said.

"Not to me. To the relay. She doesn't know where it goes from there. She doesn't know about this channel." Vox indicated the Z4 band monitor. "She knows the guild's mutual aid protocol routes to an unaffiliated technical contact who operates outside the official network. She knows that contact can receive problem reports. She sent the signal and is waiting to see whether anyone responds." A pause. "She's been sending it at four-hour intervals for two days. This is the twelfth instance."

Rook looked at the signal notation on Vox's analysis sheet. The Z8 civic systems node — Z8 was southeast, the GLMZ's administrative infrastructure district, the zone where the civic grid's operational centers ran from buildings that did not look like what they were: the neuretics registration hub, the identity database servers, the infrastructure that maintained the GLMZ's backbone. Grade 4 Civic Systems Engineers worked in Z8. They maintained systems there.

"Trace the origin," she said.

"I have been," Vox said. "The signal bounces through three nodes before it reaches the guild relay. The first bounce is inside Z8 civic infrastructure — a system node that Grade 4 engineers have routine access to. I can't get closer than that without responding to the signal, which opens a two-way exchange that the sender will be able to identify as a response." She looked at the paper. "If I respond, she knows someone is listening. If I don't, she either stops sending or the window closes — the deployment window for the backdoor is running, and if she is the engineer coerced to provide the access, she is running out of time."

"Don't respond yet," Rook said.

"No," Vox said. "I wanted to show you first."

Rook looked at the signal notation. The hand raised in a room that was not supposed to have anyone in it.

She had been running through this all night in the background of every other piece of analysis. The coerced engineer. The person who had been providing the Phase 0 access for six months and who was presumably being asked for the full grid access now. The person who had the professional credentials and the access authorization and the pressure of an entity that had been patient for six months and was now running out of patience as the deployment window approached.

The person who, when they ran out of options inside the official channels, had gone to the one channel that existed specifically because official channels fail.

"What's the signal's technical signature," she said. "The encoding."

Vox indicated a notation. "Guild standard encoding, 2221 revision. The certificate hash embedded in the signal is current — she renewed her guild membership this year. The Z8 origin node is on the third-tier access ring, which is the maintenance tier, not the administrative tier — Grade 4 engineers use it for routine system checks but it's not a front-facing access point. She is not sending from her work terminal." A pause. "She is sending from somewhere inside the infrastructure that would not flag on the standard access log."

"She knows she's being watched," Rook said.

"She knows the access logging system well enough to find a node that doesn't produce a logged entry," Vox said. "Which means she has been looking for that node, which means she has been planning this contact for long enough to do the research."

"How long can we wait before responding."

"The signal has a protocol timeout built in," Vox said. "Standard guild protocol: if the signal receives no response in forty-eight hours from first transmission, it closes. She sent the first one two days ago. She has approximately eight hours before the protocol window closes." Vox looked at the monitoring interface. "She will know the window has closed. She will not know whether the relay heard her."

"And after that?"

"I don't know. The guild protocol says she can re-open it with a new transmission, but each re-open generates a new certificate hash and the previous contact attempt is not forwarded. She would be starting over. Each re-open is also a risk exposure — more signals, more certificate hashes, more opportunity for whoever is monitoring her to notice she has been reaching outside the official network."

Rook looked at the operational board. The third column with its one entry: *neuretics registry access — coerced Grade 4 CSE — unlocated.*

The hand raised in the room. The person who had been carrying the weight of a six-month coercion relationship with an organization that had been patient for six months and was now counting down the same thirty days that Rook was counting down. The person who had looked at the options available to them and had concluded that the guild's unaffiliated technical contact relay was the least worst one.

"Eight hours," she said.

"Approximately," Vox said.

Rook looked at the table for a moment. She looked at the third column. She looked back at Vox.

"Don't respond yet," she said again. "Give me three hours to think about this."

"You have eight," Vox said.

"I know." She pulled a chair to the table and sat down. "I want to know who she is before we open the channel. What can you get from the certificate hash without responding."

"The certificate hash maps to a guild registration number," Vox said. "The guild's membership registry is not public, but the registration number format follows a predictable scheme that I can cross-reference against the GLMZ's public civic infrastructure personnel directory." She was already pulling the notation. "The public directory lists Grade 4 Civic Systems Engineers by district assignment. Z8 has fourteen Grade 4 engineers in active rotation. Narrowed by the specific third-tier access node in the signal — there are three engineers with routine authorization for that node tier."

"Three," Rook said.

"Cross-referenced against the certificate hash's registration year and the guild's Z8 chapter member list, which I can access through the gray-market archive because someone leaked it in 2223 and it has been in the archive since." She was writing. "One name."

She wrote it.

Rook read it.

*Priya Ramanujan-Cross. Grade 4, Z8 Civic Infrastructure Division. Neuretics Registration Systems, eastern sector maintenance.*

Neuretics Registration Systems.

The third entry in the Protocol's Phase 1 requirements. The person who maintained the infrastructure the Protocol needed to access. The person who had been providing the test cluster data for six months. The person the Syndicate was now leaning on for the full grid access.

The person who had found the maintenance node that didn't produce a logged access entry and had been sending a distress signal into the gray-market relay every four hours for two days.

Rook looked at the name on the paper.

She looked at the third column on the operational board.

She did not fill it in yet.

"Three hours," she said. "Let me think about how to open this correctly."

Vox nodded. She returned to the monitoring interface. The Z8 signal had eight hours in its protocol window. It had already waited two days, which meant the person sending it had been in the position she was in for at least that long — probably longer, the two days of signaling preceded by however many days of arriving at the conclusion that signaling was the only remaining option.

Rook put the name in her coat pocket — the paper, the three words. She went to the operational board and looked at the third column for a long time.

The hand raised in the room.

Someone had been waiting for someone to see it.

[Beat 22]
At 0612, Rook told Vox to open the channel.

Vox constructed the response relay — one-way from the Z4 gray-market node, bounced through the secondary thread that touched the guild's mutual aid frequency, structured to appear as a generic technical response from the unaffiliated broker rather than a direct call-back. The response was brief: *Signal received. Channel secure. Standby.* Nine words. Enough to confirm the relay was live. Not enough to identify who was on the other end of it.

Priya's response came back in eleven minutes.

Eleven minutes was fast enough that Vox noted it without comment. Fast enough that the person on the other end had been sitting at the terminal with a response prepared, waiting for confirmation that the relay was active. Eleven minutes was not the response time of someone who needed to think about whether to proceed.

"She's ready," Vox said.

"Route it through the room," Rook said.

The channel audio came through Vox's equipment — not broadcast, not speakerphone, the operational audio of a secure relay at low volume, audible to the crew at the table without being loud enough to carry. A voice: South Indian accent with Scottish inflections, the GLMZ vowel shifts of someone who had spent twenty years in the city overlaid on something that had been underneath before the city. Measured. Not calm — measured the way that people are measured when they are frightened and have decided that the fear is not going to be in charge of what happens next.

"This is Priya Ramanujan-Cross," the voice said. "I am a Grade 4 Civic Systems Engineer assigned to the Z8 neuretics registration infrastructure. I have been under coercion for six months by an organization I will identify if the channel is confirmed secure. I need help. I don't know who I'm talking to. I need to talk to someone anyway." A pause of two seconds. "Please confirm the channel."

Vox muted. She looked at Rook.

Rook said: "Confirm it."

Vox unmuted. She spoke into the relay: "Channel is secure. You are not talking to the guild administration. You are talking to an unaffiliated technical contact. We are not the oversight system. We will listen to the full brief. Take the time you need."

Another pause. Longer. Four seconds.

"All right," Priya said.

The briefing took forty minutes.

She began with the approach — the first contact from a Lotus representative six months ago. Not identified as Lotus at the time: the representative had presented as a civic infrastructure consulting firm, the kind that contracted with Grade 4 engineers for system audits, a routine engagement. She had taken the work because consulting contracts supplemented her civic service salary and because the engagement description — a neuretics registry compatibility audit for a community health pilot — was the kind of work she had done for legitimate clients before.

The first four weeks had been legitimate. She had run the audit. She had produced the report. The report identified seventeen nodes in the Z6 civic grid test cluster that met the compatibility criteria for the community health pilot.

"I found out afterward that the audit report was being used as the targeting document for the Phase 0 access," she said. "After I filed the report, the contract representative came back with a follow-on engagement: they needed me to maintain the access channel to those seventeen nodes over a six-month period for the pilot's monitoring requirements." A pause. "I agreed. The monitoring requirements looked routine. The audit had been routine. I thought the follow-on was the same."

"When did you understand it wasn't," Rook said.

"Week seven," she said. "I ran a routine check on the node access logs and found query patterns that didn't match a community health pilot. The queries were pulling heritage classification data. Not health data. The heritage field." Another pause. "I queried the GLMZ's registry to see what that field contained. It contained heritage classification entries that the Meridian Charter retired from the public census in 2211. They were still in the infrastructure database. I didn't know that before I ran the query. I suspect most Grade 4 engineers don't know it."

"What did you do," Rook said.

"I contacted the contract representative. I told them the query patterns were inconsistent with the stated engagement scope. They told me the scope had been updated — that the monitoring requirements now included heritage data for the pilot's community composition analysis." She said this with the flatness of someone describing a lie they had recognized as a lie and had not been able to name as one at the time. "I asked for documentation. They produced documentation. The documentation looked correct. It had the right signatures, the right formatting, the right authorization chain." A pause. "It was fabricated. I know that now. I didn't know it then."

The briefing continued. The escalation had happened at week twelve — the contract representative had come to her in person, not sub-vocal, in her apartment. They had named the organization. They had shown her what they had: her query access history, her node maintenance records, her technical involvement in the Phase 0 deployment. They had presented it as a two-option situation. She could continue providing the access pathway and receive the same consulting fee she had been receiving. Or the access history would go to the GLMZ Civic Bureau as evidence that she had knowingly participated in an unauthorized registry access operation.

"I hadn't knowingly participated," she said. Her voice had changed slightly — not louder, but denser, the specific quality of a person who had been carrying an injustice for long enough that the injustice was always audible when they described it, regardless of the register they tried to maintain. "I had been used to build a deployment framework I didn't know about and was then threatened with the evidence of my own use."

"But you continued providing access," Rook said.

"I continued," she said. "For six months. Because the alternative was professional termination, criminal referral, and — I believe, based on what they implied in the second meeting — worse." A pause. "I have been building the full neuretics registry backdoor since month four. Not the test cluster. The full grid. They gave me a technical specification. I have been executing it. The backdoor will give them query access to the complete GLMZ neuretics registration database — every registered neuretics user in the city, full heritage classification data, civic identity records."

The room was very quiet.

"How complete is the backdoor," Rook said.

"Ninety percent," she said. "The final deployment key needs to be inserted into the Z8 primary infrastructure node. I have been delaying this. I have been producing technical justifications for the delay — compatibility testing, security review protocols. I have run out of justifications. The deployment window — I know about the generation update, I maintain the update infrastructure, I know what the window means. The representative contacted me eight days ago. They need the final key inserted in eleven days."

"Three days before the update window opens," Rook said.

"Three days," she confirmed.

Eleven days. Not thirty. Eleven.

Rook looked at Vox. Vox had already noted the revision on the analysis paper — the timeline that was not thirty days for Priya, not the full Protocol window, just the eleven-day window before the deployment key needed to go in. After the key was in, the backdoor was live. After the backdoor was live, the Syndicate had the full grid access. What happened during the generation update window would happen regardless of whether anyone stopped the key insertion.

Eleven days.

"Who else knows what you've told me," Rook said.

A pause that was slightly longer than the others. "No one official. I have been too —" She stopped. Started again. "I tried to file a report with the GLMZ Civic Bureau in month three. I prepared the documentation. I went to the office. I sat in the waiting area." Another pause. "The representative called me while I was sitting in the waiting area. They told me they knew where I was. They told me the documentation I had prepared would be logged in the Bureau's evidence archive, which they had access to. They told me what would happen to the documentation in the archive." A pause. "I left."

"Anyone else," Rook said.

"My supervisor knows something is wrong with my schedule," she said. "I've been running unauthorized maintenance windows — using them to build the backdoor infrastructure without logging the access correctly. He's noticed the discrepancy. He hasn't filed anything yet. I think he's hoping it resolves." A pause. "It won't resolve the way he's hoping."

"Anyone outside work," Rook said.

"No," she said. "I have been carrying this alone."

The third question and three answers. How long: six months. How complete: ninety percent, eleven days to full deployment. Who else knows: nobody who can help and nobody she has been able to reach.

None of the answers were good.

Rook looked at the operational board. The third column. *Neuretics registry access — coerced Grade 4 CSE — unlocated.*

She picked up the paper from her pocket. Priya Ramanujan-Cross. Grade 4. Z8 Civic Infrastructure. She was holding the paper and looking at the board and the two things were now the same thing.

"I'm going to tell you what we are," she said, into the channel. "We're not the oversight system. We're not the Bureau. We're not going to file a report that goes into an archive the Syndicate has access to." She said this clearly, one sentence at a time, the way she said things when the thing mattered and the register needed to be exact. "We are a team that has been working this problem from a different angle for the past several weeks. We have the Lotus Syndicate's demographic baseline. We have the testimony of a witness to the Protocol's authorization vote. We need the third component. You are the third component."

Silence on the channel.

"I don't know what you mean by the third component," Priya said.

"The backdoor," Rook said. "We need to stop it from being deployed. The same way you do."

A longer silence. Six seconds. Seven.

"You know about the Protocol," Priya said.

"Yes."

Another silence. "I thought I was the only person who —" She stopped. Started again. "I thought no one outside the organization knew."

"We know," Rook said. "We've known for a few weeks. We've been looking for you for less time than that." She kept her voice even, not warm — even. The register of a person who is telling the truth and is not trying to sell it as something more than the truth. "What we need from you is specific: we need the backdoor architecture, we need the deployment pathway, and we need the eleven days you still have before the key insertion deadline. What we're going to offer in exchange is a way out of the coercion relationship that doesn't involve you going back to the Civic Bureau with documentation that will disappear into an archive the Syndicate controls."

"What way out," she said.

"We're going to take the Protocol apart from the outside," Rook said. "When that's done, the coercion relationship doesn't have a source anymore. The organization doing the coercing loses the leverage because the thing they were using you to build stops being viable." A pause. "I'm not going to pretend that the next eleven days are going to be comfortable. You're going to have to keep appearing cooperative to the Syndicate's representative while we work the other threads. If you break contact now, they accelerate the timeline. We can't have them accelerate the timeline."

"You want me to keep pretending," she said. The density in her voice again — the injustice of it, audible beneath the controlled delivery.

"For eleven days," Rook said. "After that, I am going to give you the opportunity to put what you know on the record with someone who cannot be bought."

Silence. Eight seconds. Nine.

"I've been trying for six months to find someone who cannot be bought," Priya said. "In the GLMZ." A pause. "I haven't found one."

"I know," Rook said. "That's why you sent the signal to the gray-market relay instead of the Bureau."

Another silence. Shorter.

"What do you need from me," she said.

Rook told her. The backdoor architecture: complete technical documentation of what had been built, how it interfaced with the Z8 primary node, what the final deployment key looked like and what its insertion required. The deployment pathway: the specific access window the Syndicate was expecting her to use, the timing, the authorization chain. The Syndicate's representative: how they made contact, what channel, what the escalation protocol looked like.

Priya produced all of it.

She produced it with the speed and precision of a woman who had been organizing this information in her head for a long time, knowing she would need to produce it to someone at some point, not knowing who, and keeping it organized against the possibility. Technical vocabulary throughout — the natural register of a Grade 4 Civic Systems Engineer describing infrastructure she had built and maintained and had been trapped inside for six months. Rook listened and Vox wrote and the documentation accumulated on the analysis sheet.

At the forty-minute mark, Priya said: "Is that enough?"

"Yes," Rook said.

"Will it work," she said. Not rhetorical. A genuine question, the question of someone who had been carrying something alone for six months and had just handed it to someone else and was not certain the someone else had the capacity to hold it.

"I don't know yet," Rook said. "I'm not going to tell you it will work when I don't know. What I can tell you is that we have more pieces than we had this morning, and this morning we had more than we had a week ago, and we are working on it."

A pause.

"That's the most honest thing anyone has said to me in six months," Priya said.

Rook looked at the operational board. The third column: no longer blank.

"Don't change your behavior with the representative," she said. "Continue the timeline you've been running. "I know how to stall," Priya said. There was something dry in it — not quite humor, the dryness of a person who had been stalling for six months and had learned the technique thoroughly. "I'm good at it by now."

"Good," Rook said.

"When will I hear from you again."

"Within forty-eight hours," Rook said. "The channel stays open. If something changes on your end — if the timeline accelerates, if the representative contacts you outside the scheduled window, if anything changes — you send the signal. We will respond."

The channel closed.

The room was quiet for a moment. Vox had five pages of documentation on the analysis sheet — the backdoor architecture, the deployment pathway, the representative's contact chain. Five pages on the third component, produced by the person who had been building it alone for six months in a Z8 building where her supervisor was pretending not to notice the discrepancy in the access logs.

Rook stood up from the table.

"Eleven days," she said.

"Not thirty," Vox said.

"Not thirty." She looked at the operational board. The third column. "We move faster."

Ekow looked up from the pattern review he had been running at the secondary table. He had been listening to the full briefing without comment — the specific quality of a man who had been inside the organization doing the coercing, processing the description of its methods from the position of someone who knew the methods by name. He had not interrupted. He had not added anything. He had listened.

"The representative," he said. "The contact description she gave. The channel protocol." He looked at the notes. "That's a Thorn administrative contact. "From the channel description and the contact timing, probably," he said. "I'll need an hour."

"You have it," she said.

She picked up the operational board paper and looked at the three columns. She added the notation to the third column: *Priya Ramanujan-Cross / Grade 4 / Z8 / 11 days to key insertion.* She added the deadline.

Three threads. All of them located. All of them in motion.

Twenty-six days to the generation update window. Eleven days to the backdoor key insertion, which rendered the window moot. Three jobs that were one job, one source, one plan, and now one clock that was running faster than she had calculated.

She put the paper back on the board.

"We have work to do," she said.

[Beat 23]
At 0800, Rook cleared the operational board.

Not removed it. Cleared it — took down the existing paper, folded it, set it aside, and started over with a fresh sheet on the east wall. The existing board had been accumulative, the notation of an analysis in progress, each entry added as the information arrived and understood in the context of what was known at the time. It was not wrong. It had been the correct board for the past four days. But the Priya contact had changed what the crew was looking at, and a board built for an analysis in progress was a different tool than a board built to show what was now known completely. She needed to show what was now known completely.

Ekow had identified the Thorn administrative representative in forty minutes instead of an hour. He had given her the name and the contact chain and the operational role, and she had written it on the new board in the third column, and the third column was no longer blank, and the board was now a different kind of document.

She looked at it from the other side of the room.

The surface was a section of the former textile cooperative's layout board — the cooperative had used it for pattern planning, a surface of two meters by one meter mounted on the east wall, the paper pinned at the corners, solid enough to write on with enough force to think by. It was not electronic. It had no connection to any network. It was paper and ink and the physical fact of information organized by hand, and the information it contained lived only here, visible only to the people in this room, as analog and traceable and undeniable as writing on a wall.

Three columns.

The first column ran from the heading THREAD ONE down the left side of the board. *The Headcount sculpture. Gray Horizon Collective commission, 2213. Commissioned by Z5 cultural development foundation — front entity, Lotus Syndicate administrative origin. Civic-grid schema Version 4.2 (infrastructure, not public). Heritage classification field. Below all three columns, running the width of the board, Rook had written two lines.

*DEPLOYMENT WINDOW: 26 days (generation update begins). KEY INSERTION DEADLINE: 11 days.*

*CLIENT: LOTUS SYNDICATE (anonymous via Ferris). Source: Purification Protocol operational budget. One client. One plan. Three threads.*

She looked at it from across the room.

Scout was the first to get up and stand in front of it.

He nodded once. He went back to his chair.

Ekow had not come to the board. He was at the secondary table, the Thorn pattern review still open in front of him, the documentation from the Priya briefing added alongside it. *Yim Seul-ki, 73, thirty years as Syndicate head.*

He had been looking at that entry since Rook put it on the board. He had not said anything about it.

Lace came to the board last. She stood in front of it and did not read the columns in order — she moved across the board the way she moved through a social environment, reading the connections rather than the items, the relationship between the threads rather than the threads themselves. "Which means the person who designed it has been running it for forty years," Lace said. "The Root."

She said it without inflection, as an architectural observation. The board showed the timeline. The timeline showed one consistent authorship across four decades. Forty years was not an organizational project. Forty years was a person.

Nari appeared in the doorway of the sleeping room.

She had woken up — not from sound, the safe house was quiet at this hour, but from whatever internal mechanism woke her when something in her proximity was significant. She was looking at the board from the doorway. She could not read the notation from that distance, but she could read the three columns and the timeline and the shape of the thing on the wall.

"You've mapped it," she said.

"Yes," Rook said.

Nari came into the room. She came to the board and stood in front of it and read it the way she had read the Protocol's founding document in 2211 — completely, carefully, with the attention of someone who was going to need to remember what they were looking at. She read all three columns. She read the names. She read the timeline.

She stopped at the third column.

"You've found the access pathway," she said.

"Yes."

She looked at the key insertion deadline. She looked at the deployment window. She did her own arithmetic, which took approximately four seconds.

"Eleven days," she said.

"Eleven days to the key insertion," Rook said. "Twenty-six to the generation update. The key insertion is the operational deadline."

Nari stood at the board. She was reading *Yim Seul-ki, 73, thirty years* in the second column. She had known that name for twenty-two years, since before her adjutant role, since before she understood what the Syndicate was. She had known that name the way Z6 residents knew the Syndicate's community services mark — as a fundamental fact of the environment, as something that had been present before they arrived and that organized the space around it.

"She's been running this since before I was born," Nari said.

Nobody answered this. It was not a question.

She looked at the board for another moment. Then she said, "What does the plan look like from here."

Rook looked at the board. She had been looking at the board for two hours and had not produced the plan yet, because the plan required something she did not have yet — not information, she had the information, but the specific operational design of how to apply the information in a way that produced the outcome rather than the failure mode. The information said: all three threads in hand, eleven days, a target who has been running this plan for forty years and has an entire organization's operational capacity behind her.

The plan said: you cannot take the load-bearing element out without an alternative structure in place.

"We identify the alternative load path," she said. "The community infrastructure that has to survive the Syndicate's dismantlement. We find the person inside the Syndicate who has enough authority and enough reason to make a decision the rest of the Syndicate can't reverse. We give that person a choice that is worse for them than the alternative."

"What's the alternative," Scout said.

"The documentation," Rook said. "All three threads. The Headcount data, the Nari testimony, the backdoor architecture. Simultaneously, to the GLMZ Civic Bureau and the Z5/Z6 Legal Cooperative and a public disclosure archive that Vox will establish." She indicated the board. "If the Root chooses to dismantle the Protocol publicly and fund the community infrastructure transition, the documentation does not go public. If she doesn't —"

"It goes to three places at once," Scout said.

"Three places at once," Rook confirmed. "The Meridian Charter's data equity provisions, the Legal Cooperative's case files, and a public archive. The Syndicate loses the community trust that makes the load-bearing viable. Which means the load-bearing fails regardless of whether it was dismantled or not. The only outcome where the community infrastructure survives is the outcome where the Root makes the correct decision."

Lace was looking at the board with the particular attention of a person who was computing what the plan required of her specifically. The inner sanctum. The Root. The conversation in the room where the choice was offered. She would be part of how that room was entered.

"She'll make our argument before we make it," Lace said. "Remove the Syndicate and what replaces it is worse. She'll say that."

"She'll be right," Rook said. "That's what makes the conversation difficult. The argument has to get past the correct argument to the wrong conclusion."

"And the wrong conclusion is," Lace said.

"That the correct argument justifies the Protocol," Rook said. "It doesn't. Forty years of community service does not authorize the systematic displacement of the people who depended on that service. You don't get to build the load and then use the load as the justification for taking the community apart." She looked at the board. "But she's going to make the argument, and the argument is going to be real, and the response to it has to be exact."

The board was on the wall. Three columns. One plan. One clock.

Eleven days.

Outside the secondary safe house, the Z3 street was doing its morning thing — the commercial arterial activating, the GLMZ's working population beginning its day in the zone above the basement room where six people and one witness were looking at a piece of paper on a wall that contained the complete operational picture of a forty-year plan and the eleven-day window to stop it.

"We need to move on all three threads simultaneously," Rook said. "Extract Priya from the watched building — she can't stay in place for eleven days without the timeline accelerating when the representative loses patience. Find the entry point to the inner sanctum. Build the disclosure infrastructure so it's ready to trigger if the conversation goes wrong." She looked at the crew. "Ekow has the Thorn pattern analysis. That's the entry mechanism."

He looked up from the secondary table. He had been waiting for this part of the conversation with the patience of a man who had been carrying the knowledge of how to enter a door he had spent twelve years using from the other side.

"The inner sanctum has two access points," he said. "The authorized entry — Stem members, Root staff, Thorn senior command. And the authorized absence — the administrative deployment authorization, which is a credential I have used eleven times to move inside the Syndicate's infrastructure without Stem-tier verification. The credential is mine. It has not been revoked because I am not flagged as a defector — I am flagged as an overdue deployment, which is a different status category."

"How long before the overdue status escalates to flagged," Rook said.

"I have three days," he said. "After that, the status moves and the credential fails."

Three days. Eleven days on the key insertion. Three days on the entry credential. The operational deadline was not eleven days. It was three days to enter the Syndicate's infrastructure before the window closed.

Rook looked at the board.

"We move in three days," she said.

The board was on the wall. The crew was in the room. The second safe house was a former textile cooperative with a pattern-planning surface on the east wall that had never been used for anything like this. The city was outside and did not know. The Protocol was in its final thirty days of development and did not know the crew had all three threads.

Eleven days to key insertion.

Three days to entry.

One client. One source. One plan.

One crew.

## Altitude

[Beat 24]
Lace had the credentials on the tertiary identity she had been running for six weeks: Axiom Heritage Acquisitions, a Z4 consultancy with aeroplex access authorization at the Spine. Grade 4 aeroplex clearance. Level 14 office registration. The credential set had been built for this — for a job that required legitimate presence at 480 meters, presence that read as membership rather than access, the specific social authorization of a person for whom being at altitude was unremarkable because altitude was where they worked.

The VTOL seated six. The crew boarded in cover formation — the presentation of a consulting team beginning an early-morning aeroplex session, which was exactly the kind of group that used a maintenance-registered VTOL rather than a commercial Spine transit, because consulting firms managed their access costs and the maintenance rate was sixty percent below commercial. The logic of it was unremarkable. This was the point.

Nari was not on the VTOL. She stayed at the Z3 secondary safe house — Gerald on the building exterior, Scout's threading presence in place before Rook reached the rooftop. Scout's body was at the Spine ops center. She had said: "I'll be here." And she had been in two places simultaneously before Rook reached the rooftop.

The VTOL ascended.

Rook had been in aerostatic transports before. The sensation was familiar: the Eigenlift's specific acceleration, the absence of vibration that mechanical systems produced, the altitude arriving not as a physical pressure but as a gradual reconfiguration of what was visible through the ports — the Z3 street receding, the block geometry becoming block patterns, the city resolving from the ground-level of persons and transactions into the topographical overhead of infrastructure and density. At two hundred meters, the GLMZ was a specific texture: ferrocement and the layered vertical development of four decades of building codes that pushed residential above commercial above utility. At three hundred, it was a map. At four hundred, it was a fact.

Lace was looking at the ascending view with the ease of a person who had seen it before. Not because she enjoyed altitude particularly — because she had worn identities that came here, identities with Spine aeroplex access, identities for whom the VTOL ascent was the routine beginning of a working day. The ease was professional. It was not hers.

She caught Rook's eye. "The Spine's observation decks are on levels three and seven," she said. "Level three is the public amenity tier. Level seven requires aeroplex resident credentials. The Axiom authorization covers both."

"We'll need level seven," Rook said. "For the sightline to the Z8 infrastructure district."

"I'll confirm the access window before we land."

The hum continued. The altitude continued to arrive.

At 480 meters, the VTOL landed on the Spine's Level 3 landing pad — the guest and commercial access tier, the entry point for anyone who belonged at the Spine without being a resident. The pad was clean and substantial, the ferrocement surface covered by the aeroplex's surface treatment: a pale composite material that read as finished rather than industrial, that absorbed foot traffic without showing it, that was the exact surface treatment of a place designed to signal that the people using it did not have to think about the material underneath.

The Spine aeroplex was silk at 480 meters. Not the material — the aesthetic. The surfaces were smooth. The climate management was present and unacknowledged, the way effective climate management was always present and unacknowledged in environments designed for people who did not have to think about climate management. The light was controlled: the full-spectrum output of an aeroplex lighting system that cost more per annual operating cycle than the Z6 community center's entire renovation budget, calibrated to a quality of illumination that said indoor and outdoor simultaneously, that said the altitude was not an imposition but an amenity. Corridors with sightline views of the GLMZ. Amenity nodes at every level intersection. The commercial and residential infrastructure of people who had solved the problem of where to be by being above the problem.

Boiler walked through the Level 3 corridor without comment, which was his version of commentary. His expression held the particular quality he brought to structures he was assessing: the gaze that moved across surfaces and load paths rather than aesthetics, the attention of a person who was looking at what held the thing up rather than at the thing. The Spine's structure was iron underneath the silk surface — the aeroplex's actual skeleton was one of the GLMZ's most significant engineering achievements, a multi-column anchored platform system running on the deep-pile foundation network that the aeroplex had required fifteen years to construct. He knew this. He was reading it in the surface treatment, in the column spacing visible in the corridors, in the load distribution geometry that was present in the architecture if you knew how to look for it.

"Sixty-year structure," he said quietly, to Rook. "The original platform was built in 2166. The Level 3 through 12 additions were 2198 to 2211. The Level 13 and 14 residential additions were 2219." He was doing it the way he always did it — building the structural biography of a thing he was in. "The foundation work is exceptional. The load distribution is clean." A pause. "Expensive to maintain. The maintenance contract would be the single largest operational cost on the aeroplex's budget."

"I know," Lace said, from two steps ahead. "Axiom's lease renewal included a maintenance surcharge addendum. That's how I know the Spine's Level 14 office maintenance schedule." She did not look back when she said it.

Rook followed Lace through the Level 3 corridor to the observation deck access.

The observation deck was on the south face of Level 3 — a forty-meter terrace enclosed by composite barrier rails, the barrier material combining structural load capacity with full visual transparency. The GLMZ spread below it. From 480 meters, on a clear morning, the city was everything: the full lateral extent of the Great Lakes Metropolitan Zone, the lake to the north, the zone corridor running east-west along the lakeshore, the Z3 through Z8 distribution visible in the development density patterns, the Sky People platform network scattered at altitude across the zone like a separate topography overlaid on the ground-level city.

The observation deck had benches, planters, a climate management envelope. Three other aeroplex residents were on the terrace — a couple at the east rail, a person working on a transparent display surface at the center bench. Nobody looked up when the crew appeared.

Ekow stopped at the threshold.

He stopped the way a person stops when they encounter something they had not prepared themselves for correctly. Not surprise — he had been briefed on the Spine's altitude, he knew 480 meters as a number, he had seen the aeroplex structure from below many times. The number had been abstract. The threshold was not abstract.

He stepped out onto the terrace and walked to the south rail.

He stood there.

Rook watched from the terrace threshold, not going to the rail yet. She was watching Ekow, and watching Ekow was the operational priority in this moment, because whatever was happening at the rail was something she needed to understand before the rest of the work began.

The south rail faced the Gray Zone.

From 480 meters, the Gray Zone — Z5, Z6, Z7, the buffer corridor that ran between the Zone's central districts and the outer residential zones — was a texture. A specific texture, distinct from the Z3 commercial density to the north and the Z8 administrative infrastructure to the east, distinct from the Sky People platform network overhead. The Gray Zone's texture was the texture of the ferrocement wave: the particular low-to-mid-rise density of communities that built to code and rebuilt to code and rebuilt again over forty years without the budget for anything higher than eight floors, the organic street grid of a place that had grown rather than been designed, the canal network visible from altitude as dark lines threading through the built density.

From 480 meters, the Gray Zone was a topographic feature of the city. It was not a neighborhood. It was not a community. It was not the free clinic running until midnight or the market columns load-bearing for the residential above or the debt management offices in the same institutional mark as the health workers. From 480 meters, it was a texture. It was the part of the city between the commercial core and the outer zones, visible as a zone of lower density and organic street patterns, identifiable from altitude the way you identified a geological formation — by shape, by color, by the distinctive pattern it made at a distance.

Ekow stood at the rail and looked at it.

He stood there for a long time. Three minutes. Five. Rook watched him and did not approach. The couple at the east rail had moved inside. The person at the center bench was still working. Nobody paid attention to a large man standing at the south rail, looking at the city below. People came to the observation deck to look at the city. He was looking at the city.

But what he was seeing was different from what other people saw when they looked.

He was seeing the Gap at twelve years of ground-level resolution. He knew the Z6 canal corridor because he had walked it for supply transfers. He knew the Old Harbor market district because he had stood in its lanes at night and watched what moved through them. He knew the debt management offices in the same institutional mark as the health workers because he had run debt collection routes. He knew the community — the specific topography of it, the daily operation of it, the texture of people who lived there — in a way that was only possible from the ground.

From the rail at 480 meters, none of that was visible. From the rail at 480 meters, the Gray Zone was a zone. A texture. A part of the city that had a shape and a position and a density distribution and that was not, at this altitude, inhabited. The people in it were not visible. The clinics, the markets, the maintenance corridors, the packed bags by the doors of Z6 apartments — none of it was visible. The Gray Zone from 480 meters was the same kind of feature as the lake or the highway network or the Sky People platform system: a fact of the GLMZ's geography, present, mappable, featureless.

Rook understood what Ekow was seeing.

He was seeing the Protocol's point of view. Not the founding document, not the Stem vote, not the operational language. The physical fact that made the Protocol's logic coherent: from this altitude, the people you were planning to displace were not people. They were a zone. A demographic distribution. A topology.

The Protocol had been designed from this altitude. Not literally — not the founding document, the planning sessions, the operational detail. But the moral altitude of it was this altitude. The forty-year plan for the systematic displacement of the Gray Zone's non-Lotus-eligible population had been possible to design and maintain because the planners had been operating at an elevation that made the population invisible. The silk of the aeroplex, the community services, the clinics and the markets — the silk was the mechanism that allowed the planners to tell themselves they cared about the people below while the altitude they were operating from made the people into a texture.

Ekow had worked for those planners for twelve years. He had been the iron — the ground-level operation, the hands that did the work. He had walked the canal corridors and the market lanes and the debt collection routes. He had been present in the thing that the planners, from this altitude, could only see as a topographic feature.

And now he was at the rail.

He understood what it looked like from here.

Rook thought: he had already known, in the abstract, that the people he worked for saw the Gray Zone this way. He had not known it in his body until he was standing at 480 meters looking at the texture below and finding that his twelve years of ground-level knowledge were not visible in it. Not from here. From here, nothing he knew was visible.

His hands were on the composite barrier rail. She could see his grip from across the terrace — not tight, not performance. Just the weight of a large man with his hands on a rail and his gaze on a city that was doing to him, at this altitude, exactly what forty years of planning had assumed it could be made to do.

Making people into a zone.

She walked to the rail.

She stood beside him, not speaking. She looked at the city below — the same view, the same texture, the same Gray Zone at the same distance. She had grown up below this, not in the Gray Zone but in adjacent geography, the specific class position of someone who had been in sight of both altitudes for long enough to know what each one looked like from the other's reference point. She had known this view was possible. She had known the view from this rail changed what the thing below looked like. She had known it and it still looked wrong from here, in the specific way that true things looked wrong when they were made into something smaller than they were.

"I've been down there for twelve years," Ekow said. He said it quietly, without looking at her, his gaze still on the zone below. Not explanation — the statement of a fact that had just been confirmed in a way he had not expected.

"I know," she said.

"From here," he said, "it looks like — " He stopped.

She waited.

"It looks like somewhere you manage," he said. "Not somewhere you live."

She looked at the zone below. The texture. The distribution.

"Yes," she said. "That's exactly what it looks like."

He was quiet for a moment.

"The Protocol makes more sense from here," he said. He said this with the specific revulsion of a person who has understood something they did not want to understand. "I don't mean that it's right. I mean the logic of it — why someone could sustain it for forty years — makes more sense when you understand that they've been looking at this."

"Yes," Rook said.

"It doesn't stop being wrong."

"No," she said. "It just explains why they kept calling it something else."

He looked at the Gray Zone for another thirty seconds. Then he looked away from the rail, toward the aeroplex interior where Lace was waiting with the access credentials and the Level 14 corridor layout and the three-day operational window before the Thorn administrative authorization expired.

"I'm ready," he said.

She turned from the rail with him.

Boiler was waiting at the terrace threshold, the Level 3 corridor behind him. He had been doing the structural analysis of the platform and had arrived at what he arrived at when he did structural analysis: what held, what was load-bearing, what would need to be addressed before anything else could change. He looked at them when they came off the terrace.

He did not ask what had happened at the rail. His expression said he understood something had happened and that the understanding was adequate.

"Level fourteen corridor is mapped," he said. "The maintenance access to the utility infrastructure runs through the Level 7 administrative tier. The junction we need is on the north face of Level 9." He indicated his operational notebook. "I have the load path."

"Let's go to work," Rook said.

The Eigenlift carried them up the Spine's interior shaft — the hum of it steady, the altitude increasing without ceremony, the aeroplex's floors counting upward. Below them, visible through the shaft's internal port windows, the GLMZ's topography: the zones, the textures, the distributions, the feature that looked like somewhere you managed rather than somewhere you lived.

The people in it were not visible from here.

That was the point. That had always been the point. And they were going to make it visible anyway.

[Beat 25]
Vox had the equipment unpacked in eleven minutes.

The bench workspace ran along the north wall — three meters of surface, adequate for the documentation and the relay equipment and the secondary screen she used for threaded feeds. The equipment closet was sealed and clear, the access panel keyed to the Axiom authorization credentials. No exterior windows. The Axiom lease had been built for exactly this; the absence of sightlines to the exterior was a selection criterion, not a coincidence.

She ran the channel check first. Four frequencies, sequential: the gray-market relay through the Z4 secondary node, the technical bounce through the guild's mutual aid frequency, the direct channel to Priya's personal neuretics address, and the fourth — the one that ran in parallel with everything else, the monitoring thread she maintained at four channels because she had decided to stay at four. She had been at six once. She had come back from six and rebuilt to four and chosen to stop there. The fourth channel's check took three seconds. It came back clean.

She moved to the crawlers.

Scout had the two units unpacked on the equipment closet floor and running on passive mode. They were maintenance-scale hardware — the form factor of a ventilation inspection unit, the kind of equipment the Spine's building management sent through the utility corridors on scheduled checks. Unremarkable in that environment. The corridors they would enter were full of unremarkable equipment doing routine work.

Scout was already threading. Rook could read it in her posture — the specific quality of distributed attention, the slight abstraction in her gaze that meant part of her occupied a different location. Gerald was still on the Z3 safe house exterior three hundred and forty meters below, threading the building's perimeter while Scout's physical body occupied a Level 14 office chair. The dual presence had been continuous since the VTOL boarding at 0600.

"Gerald is holding the safe house perimeter," Scout said. The detached register she used when threading — precise, slightly removed from the immediate physical. "The Spine crawlers are passive at the equipment closet threshold. I'm threading into the Level 12 service junction now."

"Grade 3 key working?" Rook asked.

"Clean access. Boiler sourced it at 0800 and it reads as valid maintenance authorization." A short pause while two locations processed simultaneously. "I'm in the utility corridor network. Forty-five minutes to the Level 11 cluster at maintenance protocol speed. I'll have eyes on the housing before the extraction channel opens."

Ekow was at the equipment closet threshold — not sitting, not working, physically present in the way he was present when his operational role was not yet active. The Spine's infrastructure was the Syndicate's infrastructure. Twelve years of operating inside it had given him an instinctive read on the environment that could not be replicated from documentation. He was there because if the building's behavior changed in a way Vox's channels wouldn't catch, he would catch it before it reached the operations room door.

Vox opened Priya's channel at 0840.

Ninety seconds for the relay to establish — the encryption handshake, the bounce through the guild frequency, the confirmation that the channel was clean in both directions. Priya's voice arrived: the South Indian accent with Scottish inflections, the GLMZ vowels of twenty years in the city, the measured register that was not calm but was controlled. The register of a woman who had decided the fear was not running this conversation.

"Channel is live," Vox said.

"I'm wired in. Clean on my end."

"The topology first," Vox said. "Level 9 through 14, civic grid nodes only. Scout's threading into the utility corridors. I need the architecture before she arrives at Level 11."

Priya walked them through it.

The Spine's civic grid infrastructure was distributed across six levels — a design requirement from the Meridian Charter's physical security provisions for neuretics registration systems. No single-node concentration. The distribution was the security: any unauthorized access attempt had to penetrate six separate node locations simultaneously, which made remote exploitation prohibitively complex. For a Grade 4 Civic Systems Engineer who had maintained the system for six years, the topology was not documentation — it was spatial knowledge, the three-dimensional understanding of where each component lived and how they connected. She described it from that understanding. She did not simplify. She spoke to Vox as a technical peer who needed the architecture.

Node by node, level by level: the Level 9 administrative nodes with read-access permissions only; the Level 10 query routing cluster that handled data request pipelines without accepting firmware modifications; the Level 11 neuretics registration cluster that served as the system's coordination hub; the Level 12 and 13 residential access nodes isolated from the primary civic infrastructure; the Level 14 operational management interface where Priya's administrative access tier lived.

"The coordination function is what makes Level 11 the key insertion point," she said. "The distributed firmware is already in place — it's running dormant across all six levels as an overlay on the legitimate architecture. The Z6 test cluster validated the design. The full grid deployment needs the coordination node to bring the distributed components online as a unified query system." A pause. "Without the key, the firmware is six separate inert components. The key goes into Level 11, activates the coordination function, and the distributed components wake up as one system."

"Physical presence requirement," Vox said.

"Meridian Charter security provisions for cluster-level coordination nodes. Firmware modifications require biometric authorization at the physical housing — it's a write-once physical lock at the hardware level. Remote installation is blocked by design." A pause. "I'm the only person with cluster authorization registered in the system. The hardware token the Syndicate delivered at month three is the key itself. It's been on my person since they gave it to me."

"The rollback procedure," Vox said.

"Same node, same physical presence requirement. The Charter's rollback mandate requires that any distributed firmware include a documented emergency shutdown path. The backdoor was designed to look like a legitimate civic grid update — it had to comply with the rollback requirement or it would fail a security audit." She said this with a specific flatness: the description of a measure intended to protect public infrastructure being used as a design constraint for the thing the measure was meant to protect against. "The rollback runs from the coordination node in fourteen minutes. I confirmed it in the Phase 0 test environment."

"The Z6 test cluster," Vox said.

A quiet of two seconds. The specific quiet of someone who had been waiting for this particular question.

"The test cluster is different," she said. "It went live eleven days ago. Three test nodes in the Z6 buffer zone — the same nodes I identified in the original audit. I thought it was technical validation. After seven days of continuous deployment, the firmware propagates to the offline backup registers on those nodes. The backup registers are not addressed by the coordination node rollback." She said this clearly, not as an apology, as a technical fact. "A rollback at Level 11 stops the full grid deployment. It does not reach the Z6 backup registers. The Phase 0 data — the 200,000 Z6 registered neuretics records — was being queried from the moment the test cluster went live. That data is already in Lotus possession. The rollback doesn't retrieve it."

Vox looked at Rook.

Rook looked at the documentation. Phase 0: 200,000 registered neuretics users in the Z6 buffer zone. Eleven days of live deployment. Heritage classification data, civic identity records, neuretics access levels, all of it in Lotus possession since day one of the test cluster. Three weeks before the crew's first job.

This was not new information. Vox had mapped the probability from the architecture three days ago. Hearing it confirmed from the person who had built the system placed it in a different register: from probability to fact. From calculation to consequence.

"The rollback at Level 11 stops the full grid deployment," Vox said.

"Yes," Priya confirmed. "The 2.3 million GLMZ neuretics registrations stay clean. The Phase 0 data is already in Lotus possession and the rollback doesn't reach it." She said this precisely, without editorial, as a person giving a complete and accurate briefing rather than the version of events she wished were true. "I know that's not complete. I know the Z6 data is already gone. I'm telling you what the rollback accomplishes."

"We stop what we can stop," Rook said.

Nobody argued. It was not an argument. It was the operational statement of a problem with a solution and a residual, and the residual was known, and the solution was still the correct action. The 2.3 million clean registrations were not nothing because 200,000 had already been compromised. That was not how the arithmetic worked.

The channel continued. Vox worked through the access topology node by node, asking the technical questions that produced the complete architecture of the distributed civic grid infrastructure across the Spine's six administrative tiers. Priya answered each question with the precision of six years of maintenance knowledge. She knew where every junction lived, which nodes had redundant access paths, which sections of the utility corridor network ran adjacent to residential units, where the cable conduit passed close enough to a maintenance access panel that a personnel approach would read as maintenance activity rather than anomalous access.

She knew this building the way a person knew a building they had been maintaining for years. Not fondly — the way you knew something you had been responsible for through three software generations and two Charter compliance audits, something you understood so completely that understanding it had stopped feeling like expertise and had become background awareness, the constant low-level read of a system you were responsible for keeping correct.

At 0932, Scout said: "I have eyes on the Level 11 cluster."

The sensor feed appeared on Vox's secondary screen — small, in the lower right corner, the view from a maintenance crawler positioned in the utility corridor above the Level 11 neuretics registration cluster's access panel. Iron maintenance corridor: cable conduit runs, load-bearing column sections, the matte-gray surface of the infrastructure the Spine's silk levels were installed over and depended on and were designed to make invisible.
